\section{Conclusion and open interface}\label{sec:conclusion}

The affine recurrence action turns one weak MCFL pumping family into a
single numerical orbit with simultaneous local returns. Strict ordering of
canonical lengths supplies monotonicity, and finite-group return times
supply congruences. Applying Broughan's adic classifications to these orbits,
together with the deleted-prime intrinsic derivative calculation, gives the
scattered-set obstruction, the nonunit escape dichotomy, and prime and
bounded-\(\Omega\) MCF-immunity.

For the minimal tail recurrence, the residual bounded-support phenomenon
has an exact structural form. It occurs on an infinite finite-fan-out MCFL
if and only if the canonical language contains a synchronized ray with
values \(cb^t\). Evertse's quotient theorem is used with an explicit
prime-ideal norm bound to reduce a fixed-support recurrence to one
characteristic root; Schur's theorem removes its polynomial factor. The
ratio \(b\) uses only primes dividing the minimal tail constant. In a
standard greedy linear numeration basis with Perron recurrence, length
thresholds then show that such a ray exists exactly for an integral dominant
root.

On the promised class of valid effectively presented regular canonical
systems, with the minimal tail recurrence and threshold supplied or
certified, positive promised instances can be enumerated and certified. The unresolved general step is not
candidate verification but witness compression: no computable bound is
known for the fan-out and word lengths of a smallest geometric synchronized
scheme. DFA path compression does not preserve the associated affine
recurrence matrices. Conversely, no reduction proving undecidability is
known that also preserves unique canonical representation, strict length
ordering, the minimal tail recurrence, and the special digit-matrix form.
Accordingly the general decision problem remains open, while the
Perron-recurrence subclass of greedy linear numeration bases is decidable.
This removes the Pisot hypothesis: strict dominance of the Perron root over
its conjugates is exactly what the greedy length argument uses.

The Supplementary Information (Online Resource 1) contains the independent fixed-DFA density and
regular Zeckendorf results. Keeping those comparisons outside the article
leaves the main theorem chain centered on synchronized recurrence orbits
and their arithmetic consequences.


 succeeded in 255ms:
\section{Synchronized pumping and recurrent numeration}
\label{sec:context-free-pisot}

We now pass from finite automata to pushdown automata. Recall that an
infinite language is \emph{CF-immune} if it contains no infinite
context-free sublanguage. Here a context-free language means one accepted
by an arbitrary nondeterministic pushdown automaton, equivalently generated
by a context-free grammar. We impose no determinism, unambiguity,
real-time, or growth hypothesis on that automaton. The classical
fixed-base theorem is due independently to Hartmanis--Shank and
Sch\"utzenberger
\cite{HartmanisShank1968,Schutzenberger1968}. The point below is that
the square-modulus block action survives the two simultaneous pumps
supplied by Ogden's lemma \cite{Ogden1968}; the interchange lemma of
Ogden--Ross--Winklmann \cite{OgdenRossWinklmann1985} is a stronger
related tool, but is not needed for the proof.

Write
\[
  \mathcal Z=\{x_1\cdots x_m\in\{0,1\}^\ast:
      x_ix_{i+1}\ne11,\ x_m=1\}
\]
and \(\val(x_1\cdots x_m)=\sum_{j=1}^m x_jF_{j+1}\).
For \(n\ge2\), let \(\omega(n)\) and \(\Omega(n)\) denote respectively
the number of distinct prime divisors and the number counted with
  multiplicity; put \(\omega(1)=\Omega(1)=0\).

\begin{lemma}[Unimodular Zeckendorf block action]
  \label{lem:main-zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}
  After an LSD-first prefix \(x_1\cdots x_r\), put
  \[
    V_r=\begin{pmatrix}N_r\\F_{r+1}\\F_{r+2}\end{pmatrix},
    \qquad N_r=\sum_{j=1}^r x_jF_{j+1},
    \qquad V_0=(0,1,1)^t.
  \]
  Reading \(\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}\) sends \(V_r\) to
  \[
    D_\varepsilon V_r,
    \qquad
    D_\varepsilon=
    \begin{pmatrix}
      1&0&\varepsilon\\
      0&0&1\\
      0&1&1
    \end{pmatrix},
    \qquad \det D_\varepsilon=-1.
  \]
  For a block \(y=y_1\cdots y_\ell\), therefore,
  \(M_y=D_{y_\ell}\cdots D_{y_1}\) has determinant
  \((-1)^\ell\), concatenation acts by multiplication of the block
  matrices, and every nonempty \(M_y\) belongs to
  \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\) for every \(q\ge2\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  The identities
  \[
    N_{r+1}=N_r+\varepsilon F_{r+2},\qquad
    F_{r+2}=F_{r+2},\qquad F_{r+3}=F_{r+1}+F_{r+2}
  \]
  give the displayed action. Expanding along the first row gives
  \(\det D_\varepsilon=-1\); the remaining assertions follow by
  multiplication and reduction modulo \(q\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Paired Zeckendorf pumping congruence]
  \label{lem:zeckendorf-paired-pumping-congruence}
  Let \(u,v,x,y,z\in\{0,1\}^\ast\), and put
  \[
    N(k):=\val(uv^kxy^kz)\qquad(k\ge0).
  \]
  Suppose that \(\abs v+\abs y>0\). If \(q\ge2\), and \(h\) is a
  common multiple of the orders modulo \(q\) of the nonempty-block
  matrices among \(M_v,M_y\) (with \(M_\varepsilon=I\)), then
  \[
    N(k+h)\equiv N(k)\pmod q
    \qquad(k\ge0).
  \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  With \(v_0=(0,1,1)^t\), concatenation and
  \Cref{lem:main-zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action} give
  \[
    N(k)=e_1^tM_zM_y^kM_xM_v^kM_uv_0.
  \]
  Every nonempty block matrix is invertible modulo \(q\). By the
  definition of \(h\), both \(M_v^h\equiv I\pmod q\) and
  \(M_y^h\equiv I\pmod q\), where the same identities are automatic for
  an empty block. Substitution in the displayed product proves the
  congruence.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Context-free coprime-quotient Zeckendorf pumping]
  \label{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}
  Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be context-free. There is an integer
  \(P_L\ge1\) such that every \(w\in L\) with
  \(\abs w\ge P_L\) and \(\val(w)\ge2\) begins a sequence
  \(w_0=w,w_1,w_2,\ldots\) in \(L\) whose lengths are strictly
  increasing and whose values \(N_i=\val(w_i)\) satisfy
  \[
    N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i>1,\qquad
    Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i},\qquad \gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Apply Ogden's lemma with every position distinguished. It supplies a
  constant \(P_L\) such that each \(w\in L\) of length at least \(P_L\)
  has a factorisation
  \[
    w=uvxyz,\qquad \abs v+\abs y>0,\qquad
    uv^kxy^kz\in L\quad(k\ge0).
  \]
  The additional marked-position bound in Ogden's lemma is not needed
  here. Starting with \(w_i\in L\), choose such a factorisation and
  let \(h_i\) be a common multiple of the orders of \(M_v\) and \(M_y\)
  in
  \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/N_i^2\ZZ)\), omitting an empty block. These
  orders exist by
  \Cref{lem:main-zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}. Define
  \[
    w_{i+1}:=uv^{\,1+h_i}xy^{\,1+h_i}z.
  \]
  By \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-paired-pumping-congruence},
  \(N_{i+1}\equiv N_i\pmod {N_i^2}\). Hence
  \(N_{i+1}=N_i(1+t_iN_i)\) for an integer \(t_i\).

  Since \(h_i\ge1\) and \(\abs v+\abs y>0\), the new word is longer.
  Both words belong to \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), and canonical
  Zeckendorf intervals are strictly ordered by length; consequently
  \(N_{i+1}>N_i\), so \(t_i\ge1\). Taking
  \(Q_i=1+t_iN_i\) gives all four assertions. The new word still has
  length at least \(P_L\) and value at least \(2\), so the construction
  iterates.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Zeckendorf CF-immunity package]
  \label{cor:zeckendorf-context-free-immunity-package}
  Every infinite context-free \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) satisfies
  \[
    \sup_{w\in L}\omega(\val(w))=\infty.
  \]
  Consequently, for each \(K\ge1\), the bounded-distinct-prime language
  \(\mathcal B_K\), the bounded-almost-prime language \(\mathcal A_K\),
  and every infinite finite-prime-support language \(\mathcal Z_S\)
  are CF-immune. In particular, the Zeckendorf prime language
  \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) is CF-immune.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Choose a sufficiently long word in \(L\) and apply
  \Cref{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}. Since
  \(N_j\mid N_i\) for \(j\le i\), while every \(Q_i>1\) is coprime to
  \(N_i\), a prime divisor of \(Q_i\) divides no earlier value. Thus
  \(\omega(N_i)\ge\omega(N_0)+i\). The immunity conclusions follow
  exactly as in the regular cases: \(\omega\le K\) on \(\mathcal B_K\),
  \(\mathcal A_K\subseteq\mathcal B_K\), and
  \(\omega\le\abs S\) on \(\mathcal Z_S\). Finally prime values have
  \(\omega=1\), while Euclid's theorem makes
  \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) infinite.
\end{proof}

We next isolate the precise base-independent interface. This is
deliberately narrower than every object called a Pisot or abstract
numeration system in the literature.

\begin{definition}[Canonical eventually recurrent numeration]
  \label{def:canonical-eventually-recurrent-numeration}
  A canonical eventually recurrent numeration system consists of:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(U\arabic*)}]
    \item a strictly increasing sequence of positive integers
    \(U=(U_n)_{n\ge0}\) and a finite digit alphabet
    \(A\subseteq\NN\) containing \(0\);
    \item an LSD-first language \(\mathcal R_U\subseteq A^\ast\), with
    \[
      \val_U(x_0\cdots x_{m-1})
        :=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}x_jU_j,
    \]
    for which \(\val_U:\mathcal R_U\to\NN_{\ge1}\) is a bijection;
    \item strict length ordering: if \(r,s\in\mathcal R_U\) and
    \(\abs r<\abs s\), then \(\val_U(r)<\val_U(s)\);
    \item integers \(J\ge0,d\ge1,a_0,\ldots,a_{d-1}\), with
    \(a_0\ne0\), such that
    \[
      U_{n+d}=a_0U_n+a_1U_{n+1}+\cdots+a_{d-1}U_{n+d-1}
      \qquad(n\ge J).
    \]
  \end{enumerate}
  Relative to this recurrence witness, it is a \emph{unit system} if
  \(\abs{a_0}=1\). No regularity of \(\mathcal R_U\) is included in
  this definition.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Affine recurrence block action]
  \label{lem:affine-recurrence-block-action}
  Under \textup{(U4)}, let \(C\) be the \(d\times d\) companion matrix
  with superdiagonal entries \(1\) and last row
  \((a_0,\ldots,a_{d-1})\). For \(\varepsilon\in A\), put
  \[
    D_\varepsilon=
    \begin{pmatrix}
      1&\varepsilon e_1^t\\
      0&C
    \end{pmatrix}.
  \]
  Once \(r\ge J\), appending \(\varepsilon\) sends
  \[
    (N_r,U_r,\ldots,U_{r+d-1})^t
    \quad\hbox{to}\quad
    D_\varepsilon(N_r,U_r,\ldots,U_{r+d-1})^t.
  \]
  Moreover
  \(\det D_\varepsilon=(-1)^{d-1}a_0\). Hence every block action is
  invertible modulo \(q\) whenever \(\gcd(q,a_0)=1\), and modulo every
  \(q\ge2\) in a unit system. Here and below, for a block
  \(b=b_1\cdots b_\ell\), we write
  \(M_b=D_{b_\ell}\cdots D_{b_1}\), with \(M_\varepsilon=I\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  The first coordinate becomes \(N_r+\varepsilon U_r\); the remaining
  coordinates shift once and the final one is supplied by \textup{(U4)}.
  These are exactly the displayed matrix equations. The determinant
  is \(\det C=(-1)^{d-1}a_0\). Multiplicativity of determinants gives
  the assertion for blocks.
\end{proof}

The coefficient \(a_0\) belongs to the displayed recurrence witness,
not intrinsically to \((U,A,\mathcal R_U)\); see
\Cref{prop:recurrence-witness-separation} for the resulting strict
separation.

We now use the standard fan-out convention: a multiple context-free
language (MCFL) is a \(k\)-MCFL for some finite \(k\ge1\). An infinite
language is \emph{MCF-immune} if it contains no infinite MCFL
sublanguage. The weak pumping lemma of Seki--Matsumura--Fujii--Kasami
states that an infinite \(k\)-MCFL contains one word that can be pumped
synchronously at \(2k\) factors \cite[Lemma~3.2]{SekiEtAl1991}. The
  lemma makes no assertion about every sufficiently long word; that
  stronger form fails for general MCFLs
  \cite{KanazawaKobeleMichaelisSalvatiYoshinaka2014}.

  Duncan--Elder--Frenkel--Lyu's substitution lemma
  \cite[Theorem~4.5]{DuncanElderFrenkelLiu2026} is stronger in a different
  quantifier direction: after enough positions of each sufficiently long
  word are marked, it gives either a bounded pump or a factorization in
  which a tuple can be replaced by another tuple from one of finitely many
  switchable families. It does not select a single family parameterized by
  one exponent \(t\), nor does it make all replacement blocks simultaneous
  powers of fixed words. Consequently it supplies neither a fixed affine
  matrix-power expression nor a return time valid for every parameter on
  one orbit. The switchable alternative also prevents it from replacing the
  paired Ogden pump in the Zeckendorf argument above. Thus it does not by
  itself imply any immunity result proved here. We use Seki et al.'s weaker
  conclusion precisely because its one synchronized family has the fixed
  \(2k\)-block power form needed below. The imported language statement is
  only that family; its placement beyond the recurrence transient, the
  finite-group return, and all arithmetic consequences are proved here.

\begin{theorem}[Synchronized local-congruence orbit]
  \label{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}
  Let \((U,A,\mathcal R_U)\) satisfy \textup{(U1)--(U4)}, and let
  \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) be an infinite \(k\)-MCFL. There are words
  \[
    \begin{gathered}
      a,u_1,\ldots,u_{k+1},v_1,\ldots,v_k\in A^\ast,\\
      w_1,\ldots,w_k,s_1,\ldots,s_k\in A^\ast
    \end{gathered}
  \]
  such that
  \[
    W(t)=a u_1v_1^tw_1s_1^tu_2\cdots
          u_kv_k^tw_ks_k^tu_{k+1}\in L
          \qquad(t\ge0),
  \]
  \[
    \sum_{j=1}^k(\abs{v_j}+\abs{s_j})>0,
  \]
  and \(N(t):=\val_U(W(t))\) is strictly increasing. Moreover, for
  every \(q\ge2\) with \(\gcd(q,a_0)=1\), there is \(H(q)\ge1\) such
  that
  \[
    N(t+H(q))\equiv N(t)\pmod q
    \qquad\text{for every }t\ge0.
  \]
  The factorization, and hence the orbit \(W(t)\), is independent of
  \(q\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Delete the finitely many words of length less than \(J\). Since the
  alphabet is finite, some \(a\in A^J\) is the prefix of infinitely many
  remaining words; when \(J=0\), take \(a=\varepsilon\). For completeness,
  fixed left quotient preserves fan-out. Introduce a fresh letter \(\#\),
  let \(h(\#)=a\) and \(h(x)=x\) for \(x\in A\), intersect
  \(h^{-1}(L)\) with the regular language \(\#A^*\), and erase \(\#\).
  Inverse homomorphism, regular intersection, and homomorphism preserve
  \(k\)-MCFLs without increasing the maximum nonterminal dimension
  \cite[Proposition~2.3]{DuncanElderFrenkelLiu2026}. Hence
  \[
    L_a=\{x\in A^\ast:ax\in L\}
  \]
  is an infinite \(k\)-MCFL. The weak pumping lemma supplies
  \(u_j,v_j,w_j,s_j\) with the displayed synchronized pumping property
  and with at least one \(v_j\) or \(s_j\) nonempty.

  All digit actions after \(a\) are in the stationary range of
  \textup{(U4)}. Fix an allowed modulus \(q\). By
  \Cref{lem:affine-recurrence-block-action}, every nonempty matrix among
  \[
    M_{v_1},M_{s_1},\ldots,M_{v_k},M_{s_k}
  \]
  lies in the finite group
  \(\operatorname{GL}_{d+1}(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\). One may take
  \[
    H(q)=\abs{\operatorname{GL}_{d+1}(\ZZ/q\ZZ)}
      \le q^{(d+1)^2};
  \]
  Lagrange's theorem makes this a common multiple of their orders, and
  empty blocks contribute the identity.
  Replacing \(t\) by \(t+H(q)\) in the matrix product for \(W(t)\)
  therefore leaves every powered block unchanged modulo \(q\), proving
  the congruence simultaneously for all \(t\ge0\).

  Finally,
  \[
    \abs{W(t+1)}-\abs{W(t)}
      =\sum_{j=1}^k(\abs{v_j}+\abs{s_j})>0.
  \]
  Both words are canonical, so \textup{(U3)} gives
  \(N(t+1)>N(t)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Explicit grammar for a synchronized ray]
  \label{lem:synchronized-ray-linear-mcfg}
  Fix \(k\ge1\), a word \(a\), and words
  \(u_1,\ldots,u_{k+1},v_1,\ldots,v_k,w_1,\ldots,w_k,
  s_1,\ldots,s_k\) over a finite alphabet. If
  \[
    \sum_{j=1}^k(\abs{v_j}+\abs{s_j})>0,
  \]
  then
  \[
    \left\{a u_1v_1^tw_1s_1^tu_2\cdots
      u_kv_k^tw_ks_k^tu_{k+1}:t\ge0\right\}
  \]
  is an infinite linear \(2k\)-MCFL.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Take a nonterminal \(X\) of fan-out \(2k\) and a start symbol \(T\) of
  fan-out one. The productions are
  \[
    X(\varepsilon,\ldots,\varepsilon)\leftarrow,
  \]
  \[
    X(v_1x_1,s_1x_2,\ldots,v_kx_{2k-1},s_kx_{2k})
      \leftarrow X(x_1,\ldots,x_{2k}),
  \]
  and
  \[
    T(a u_1x_1w_1x_2u_2\cdots
      u_kx_{2k-1}w_kx_{2k}u_{k+1})
      \leftarrow X(x_1,\ldots,x_{2k}).
  \]
  After \(t\) recursive applications, the tuple derived from \(X\) is
  \((v_1^t,s_1^t,\ldots,v_k^t,s_k^t)\), so the generated language is
  exactly the displayed ray. Every variable occurs exactly once in each
  production. The positive total pumped length makes the words at
  distinct parameters have distinct lengths.
\end{proof}

Put \(S=\{p:p\text{ is prime and }p\mid a_0\}\). On
\(\NN_{\ge1}\), let \(\tau_S\) be the topology generated by the basis
\[
  \{(n+q\ZZ)\cap\NN_{\ge1}:n\ge1,\ q\ge1,\ \gcd(q,a_0)=1\}.
\]
Its completion is
\[
  \widehat{\ZZ}_{S^c}
    :=\varprojlim_{\gcd(q,a_0)=1}\ZZ/q\ZZ
    \cong\prod_{p\notin S}\ZZ_p.
\]
These are the adic topologies and completions studied by Broughan
\cite{Broughan2003}. His Theorem~2.4 and Corollary~3.1 supply the
\(\mathbb Q\)-space and Cantor-completion classifications used below. The
new assertion here is that every synchronized MCFL orbit satisfies their
no-isolated-point hypothesis, and hence yields the scattered-set
obstruction.

\begin{theorem}[Adic recurrence and scattered-set obstruction for synchronized MCFL orbits]
  \label{thm:mcfl-local-adic-fingerprint}
  For the orbit \(Y=\{N(t):t\ge0\}\) supplied by
  \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}, the following hold.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
    \item The subspace \(Y\subseteq(\NN_{\ge1},\tau_S)\) has no isolated
    points and is homeomorphic to \(\mathbb Q\).
    \item Its closure in \(\widehat{\ZZ}_{S^c}\) is homeomorphic to the
    Cantor set.
    \item If \(B\subseteq\NN_{\ge1}\) is scattered in \(\tau_S\), then
    \[
      \operatorname{Rep}_U(B)
        :=\{w\in\mathcal R_U:\val_U(w)\in B\}
    \]
    is MCF-immune whenever it is infinite.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Let \(N(t)\in Y\), and let its basic neighborhood be specified modulo
  an allowed \(q\). If \(q=1\), take any later orbit point. If \(q\ge2\),
  \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit} gives
  \(N(t+H(q))\equiv N(t)\pmod q\), and strict increase makes these points
  distinct. Thus \(Y\) has no isolated point.

  The allowed congruences separate distinct positive integers: for
  \(m\ne n\), choose a prime outside the finite set \(S\) that does not
  divide \(m-n\). Consequently \(\tau_S\) is Hausdorff and metrizable;
  it also has a countable basis. Hence \(Y\) is a countable metrizable
  space without isolated points. The Sierpi\'nski characterization used
  in \cite[Theorem~2.4]{Broughan2003} yields \(Y\cong\mathbb Q\).

  The profinite ring \(\widehat{\ZZ}_{S^c}\) is compact, metrizable, and
  zero-dimensional. Its closed subspace \(\overline Y\) has no isolated
  point: an isolated point of the closure would, by density of \(Y\),
  belong to and be isolated in \(Y\). Thus \(\overline Y\) is a nonempty
  compact perfect zero-dimensional metrizable space, and the classical
  Cantor-space characterization gives the second assertion; compare
  \cite[Corollary~3.1]{Broughan2003}.

  For the last assertion, an infinite MCFL sublanguage of
  \(\operatorname{Rep}_U(B)\) would, by the first assertion, provide a
  nonempty subspace \(Y\subseteq B\) without isolated points. This
  contradicts scatteredness, which is hereditary to subspaces.
\end{proof}

For a prime \(p\), write \(v_p(n)\) for the exponent of \(p\) in \(n\),
and let \(\omega_{S^c}(n)=\#\{p\notin S:p\mid n\}\).
For \(K\ge0\) and \(E=(E_p)_{p\in S}\in\NN_0^S\), define
\[
  X_{K,E}=\{n\ge1:\omega_{S^c}(n)\le K,
               \ v_p(n)\le E_p\ (p\in S)\}.
\]
For \(X\subseteq\NN_{\ge1}\), let \(D_SX\) be the set of points of \(X\)
that are not isolated in its \(\tau_S\)-subspace topology. Thus \(D_S\)
is the intrinsic Cantor--Bendixson derivative. We use the height convention
\[
  \operatorname{ht}(X)=\min\{\alpha:D_S^\alpha X=\varnothing\}.
\]

\begin{proposition}[Exact local prime-factor rank]
  \label{prop:local-prime-factor-cb-rank}
  For every \(K\ge1\),
  \[
    D_SX_{K,E}=X_{K-1,E}.
  \]
  Consequently
  \[
    D_S^{\,j}X_{K,E}=X_{K-j,E}\quad(0\le j\le K),
    \qquad D_S^{\,K+1}X_{K,E}=\varnothing,
  \]
  and the Cantor--Bendixson height of \(X_{K,E}\) is exactly \(K+1\).
  For \(K=0\), the nonempty set \(X_{0,E}\) is finite,
  \(D_SX_{0,E}=\varnothing\), and its height is \(1\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Suppose first that \(n\in X_{K-1,E}\), and let \(q\ge1\) be coprime
  to \(a_0\). Dirichlet's theorem supplies a prime
  \(r\equiv1\pmod q\) outside the finite set \(S\cup\{p:p\mid n\}\).
  Then \(nr\ne n\), \(nr\equiv n\pmod q\), the \(S\)-adic valuations
  are unchanged, and
  \[
    \omega_{S^c}(nr)=\omega_{S^c}(n)+1\le K.
  \]
  Hence \(n\in D_SX_{K,E}\).

  Conversely, let \(n\in X_{K,E}\) have
  \(\omega_{S^c}(n)=K\), and write
  \[
    n=c\prod_{i=1}^K\ell_i^{e_i},
    \qquad c=\prod_{p\in S}p^{f_p},\quad 0\le f_p\le E_p,
  \]
  with distinct \(\ell_i\notin S\). Put
  \(q_0=\prod_i\ell_i^{e_i+1}\). If \(m\in X_{K,E}\) and
  \(m\equiv n\pmod {q_0}\), then
  \(v_{\ell_i}(m)=e_i\) for every \(i\). The budget of \(K\) primes
  outside \(S\) leaves no further such prime, so \(m\) belongs to the
  finite set
  \[
    F=\left\{
      \left(\prod_{p\in S}p^{g_p}\right)
      \prod_{i=1}^K\ell_i^{e_i}:
      0\le g_p\le E_p
    \right\}.
  \]
  Choose a prime \(r\notin S\cup\{\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_K\}\) dividing
  none of the finitely many nonzero differences \(m-n\), \(m\in F\).
  Then \(q=q_0r\) is coprime to \(a_0\), and the \(q\)-neighborhood of
  \(n\) meets \(X_{K,E}\) only at \(n\). Thus precisely the points with
  fewer than \(K\) outside primes survive one derivative. Iteration
  proves the formula. Finally \(X_{0,E}\) is a nonempty finite set (it
  contains \(1\)), so the height is exactly \(K+1\).
\end{proof}

Broughan's related full-adic result counts prime factors with
multiplicity and identifies closures of the corresponding strata
\cite[Theorem~4.3]{Broughan2003}. The preceding proposition is a local
refinement of that analysis, not a new classification of the ambient adic
space: it uses distinct prime support, deletes the finite set \(S\), imposes
bounded \(S\)-adic valuations, and computes derivatives intrinsically in
the indicated subspace rather than only in its ambient closure.

\begin{theorem}[Nonunit escape dichotomy and MCF-immunity]
  \label{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}
  Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) be an infinite MCFL. Then either
  \[
    \sup_{w\in L}\omega_{S^c}(\val_U(w))=\infty,
  \]
  or there is a prime \(p\mid a_0\) such that
  \[
    \sup_{w\in L}v_p(\val_U(w))=\infty.
  \]
  Consequently the following languages are MCF-immune whenever they are
  infinite:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
    \item the prime representation language \(\mathcal P_U\);
    \item every language on which \(\Omega(\val_U(w))\) is bounded;
    \item every finite-prime-support language on which all
    \(v_p(\val_U(w))\), \(p\mid a_0\), are bounded.
  \end{enumerate}
  In a unit system, every bounded-\(\omega(\val_U(w))\) language is
  MCF-immune.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  If neither alternative held, finiteness of \(S\) would give
  \(K\ge0\) and \(E\in\NN_0^S\) such that
  \[
    \{\val_U(w):w\in L\}\subseteq X_{K,E}.
  \]
  By \Cref{prop:local-prime-factor-cb-rank}, \(X_{K,E}\) is scattered.
  This contradicts
  \Cref{thm:mcfl-local-adic-fingerprint}. Prime values satisfy
  \(\omega_{S^c}\le1\) and \(v_p\le1\); bounded \(\Omega\) bounds both
  alternatives; and finite prime support bounds \(\omega_{S^c}\), with
  the remaining valuations bounded by hypothesis. If \(S=\varnothing\),
  the second alternative is absent, giving the unit-system assertion.
\end{proof}

The second alternative is necessary. In ordinary base \(b\), where
\(a_0=b\), the regular LSD-first language
\(\{0^n1:n\ge0\}\) represents \(b^n\). Its distinct prime support is
fixed, while \(v_p(b^n)\) is unbounded for every \(p\mid b\).

\begin{proposition}[Recurrence-witness inflation and strict separation]
  \label{prop:recurrence-witness-separation}
  If a tail has minimal recurrence polynomial \(\mu_U\), then for every
  prime \(p\) it also has a recurrence witness with characteristic
  polynomial \((X-p)\mu_U(X)\).  When \(\abs{\mu_U(0)}=1\), the trailing
  coefficient of this witness has absolute value \(p\).

  Moreover, there are two canonical eventually recurrent
  numeration systems with regular representation languages and recurrence
  witnesses whose trailing-coefficient prime set is \(\{p\}\), but with
  opposite bounded-outside-prime escape behavior:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
    \item ordinary base \(p\) contains the infinite regular language
    \(\{0^n1:n\ge0\}\), whose values are \(p^n\);
    \item the Zeckendorf system, with \(F_0=0,F_1=1\) and
    \(F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_n\), equipped with the order-three recurrence
    witness
    \[
      F_{n+3}=-pF_n+(1-p)F_{n+1}+(p+1)F_{n+2},
    \]
    contains no infinite MCFL \(L\) for which
    \[
      \sup_{w\in L}\omega_{\{p\}^c}(\val(w))<\infty.
    \]
  \end{enumerate}
  Thus the prime divisors of an arbitrarily selected trailing coefficient
  do not determine the escape behavior of the numeration system.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  The polynomial identity \(\mu_U(C)z_J=0\) remains valid after
  multiplication by \(X-p\).  In the companion convention, the trailing
  recurrence coefficient is the negative of the constant term of the
  characteristic polynomial, proving the first assertion.

  The base-\(p\) assertion is the preceding example.  For the Zeckendorf
  sequence, multiplication of its minimal polynomial by \(X-p\) gives
  \[
    (X-p)(X^2-X-1)
      =X^3-(p+1)X^2+(p-1)X+p,
  \]
  which is exactly the displayed recurrence and has trailing coefficient
  \(-p\).  This changes neither the sequence nor its regular canonical
  representation language.  On the other hand, its minimal recurrence
  has unit trailing coefficient.  If the displayed supremum were bounded
  by \(K\), then
  \(\omega(\val(w))\le K+1\) on \(L\), contrary to the unit-recurrence
  conclusion of
  \Cref{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}.  This proves the separation.
\end{proof}

Let
\[
  \mu_U(X)=X^{d_{\mathrm r}}-c_{d_{\mathrm r}-1}X^{d_{\mathrm r}-1}
    -\cdots-c_1X-c_0
\]
be the minimal recurrence polynomial of a tail of \(U\). Since it
divides the characteristic polynomial of a recurrence allowed in
\textup{(U4)}, its constant term is nonzero. Enlarge the recurrence
threshold to \(J_{\mathrm r}\) so that this minimal recurrence holds for
every \(n\ge J_{\mathrm r}\), and put
\[
  S_{\mathrm r}=\{p:p\text{ is prime and }p\mid c_0\}.
\]

\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{amsart}

\input{preamble}

\title[Prime support in recurrent numeration]{Prime support and multiple-context-free languages in recurrent numeration}

\author{Haobo Ma}
\address[Haobo Ma]{AELF PTE LTD., \#14-02, Marina Bay Financial Centre Tower 1, 8 Marina Blvd, Singapore 018981}
\email[Haobo Ma]{auric@aelf.io}

\author{Wenlin Zhang}
\address[Wenlin Zhang]{National University of Singapore, Singapore}
\email[Wenlin Zhang]{e1327962@u.nus.edu}
\thanks{Corresponding author: Wenlin Zhang.}

\date{}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
Let \(U\) be a strictly increasing sequence of positive integers that is
eventually linearly recurrent, and suppose that a finite digit language
gives unique representations strictly ordered by length. Combining the
weak pumping lemma for multiple context-free languages with the affine
recurrence action, we show that every infinite multiple context-free
sublanguage contains one strictly increasing synchronized orbit returning
modulo every integer coprime to the recurrence's trailing coefficient.
Applying Broughan's adic classifications and a deleted-prime intrinsic
Cantor--Bendixson calculation yields scattered-set obstructions, prime and
bounded-\(\Omega\) MCF-immunity, and, in the unit case, quotient chains of
arbitrary congruence depth and an induced countably branching divisibility
tree.

For the intrinsic minimal tail recurrence we prove an inverse theorem:
bounded prime support away from its constant term occurs on an infinite
MCFL if and only if the canonical language contains a synchronized ray
whose values are \(cb^t\) with integers \(c\ge1\), \(b\ge2\). The
arithmetic rigidity step is deduced explicitly from Evertse's theorem on
prime ideals in quotients of nondegenerate recurrences. On the promised
class of valid presentations, with a DFA and a supplied or effectively
certified minimal tail recurrence, positive instances are semidecidable;
the missing uniform witness bound for a general decision procedure is isolated. For
standard greedy linear numeration bases with Perron recurrence the problem
is decidable and is positive exactly when the dominant Perron number is an
integer. Thus the Pisot hypothesis can be replaced by the weaker
strict-dominance hypothesis.
\end{abstract}

\subjclass[2020]{68Q45, 11B37, 11A63, 37B10}
\keywords{Multiple-context-free language, linear recurrence, linear numeration,
prime support, congruence topology, Cantor--Bendixson rank}

 145:   \(\mathcal B_K\), the bounded-almost-prime language \(\mathcal A_K\),
 146:   and every infinite finite-prime-support language \(\mathcal Z_S\)
 147:   are CF-immune. In particular, the Zeckendorf prime language
 148:   \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) is CF-immune.
 149: \end{corollary}
 150: 
 151: \begin{proof}
 152:   Choose a sufficiently long word in \(L\) and apply
 153:   \Cref{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}. Since
 154:   \(N_j\mid N_i\) for \(j\le i\), while every \(Q_i>1\) is coprime to
 155:   \(N_i\), a prime divisor of \(Q_i\) divides no earlier value. Thus
 156:   \(\omega(N_i)\ge\omega(N_0)+i\). The immunity conclusions follow
 157:   exactly as in the regular cases: \(\omega\le K\) on \(\mathcal B_K\),
 158:   \(\mathcal A_K\subseteq\mathcal B_K\), and
 159:   \(\omega\le\abs S\) on \(\mathcal Z_S\). Finally prime values have
 160:   \(\omega=1\), while Euclid's theorem makes
 161:   \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) infinite.
 162: \end{proof}
 163: 
 164: We next isolate the precise base-independent interface. This is
 165: deliberately narrower than every object called a Pisot or abstract
 166: numeration system in the literature.
 167: 
 168: \begin{definition}[Canonical eventually recurrent numeration]
 169:   \label{def:canonical-eventually-recurrent-numeration}
 170:   A canonical eventually recurrent numeration system consists of:
 171:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(U\arabic*)}]
 172:     \item a strictly increasing sequence of positive integers
 173:     \(U=(U_n)_{n\ge0}\) and a finite digit alphabet
 174:     \(A\subseteq\NN\) containing \(0\);
 175:     \item an LSD-first language \(\mathcal R_U\subseteq A^\ast\), with
 176:     \[
 177:       \val_U(x_0\cdots x_{m-1})
 178:         :=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}x_jU_j,
 179:     \]
 180:     for which \(\val_U:\mathcal R_U\to\NN_{\ge1}\) is a bijection;
 181:     \item strict length ordering: if \(r,s\in\mathcal R_U\) and
 182:     \(\abs r<\abs s\), then \(\val_U(r)<\val_U(s)\);
 183:     \item integers \(J\ge0,d\ge1,a_0,\ldots,a_{d-1}\), with
 184:     \(a_0\ne0\), such that
 185:     \[
 186:       U_{n+d}=a_0U_n+a_1U_{n+1}+\cdots+a_{d-1}U_{n+d-1}
 187:       \qquad(n\ge J).
 188:     \]
 189:   \end{enumerate}
 190:   Relative to this recurrence witness, it is a \emph{unit system} if
 191:   \(\abs{a_0}=1\). No regularity of \(\mathcal R_U\) is included in
 192:   this definition.
 193: \end{definition}
 194: 
 195: \begin{lemma}[Affine recurrence block action]
 196:   \label{lem:affine-recurrence-block-action}
 197:   Under \textup{(U4)}, let \(C\) be the \(d\times d\) companion matrix
 198:   with superdiagonal entries \(1\) and last row
 199:   \((a_0,\ldots,a_{d-1})\). For \(\varepsilon\in A\), put
 200:   \[
 201:     D_\varepsilon=
 202:     \begin{pmatrix}
 203:       1&\varepsilon e_1^t\\
 204:       0&C
 205:     \end{pmatrix}.
 206:   \]
 207:   Once \(r\ge J\), appending \(\varepsilon\) sends
 208:   \[
 209:     (N_r,U_r,\ldots,U_{r+d-1})^t
 210:     \quad\hbox{to}\quad
 211:     D_\varepsilon(N_r,U_r,\ldots,U_{r+d-1})^t.
 212:   \]
 213:   Moreover
 214:   \(\det D_\varepsilon=(-1)^{d-1}a_0\). Hence every block action is
 215:   invertible modulo \(q\) whenever \(\gcd(q,a_0)=1\), and modulo every
 216:   \(q\ge2\) in a unit system. Here and below, for a block
 217:   \(b=b_1\cdots b_\ell\), we write
 218:   \(M_b=D_{b_\ell}\cdots D_{b_1}\), with \(M_\varepsilon=I\).
 219: \end{lemma}
 220: 
 221: \begin{proof}
 222:   The first coordinate becomes \(N_r+\varepsilon U_r\); the remaining
 223:   coordinates shift once and the final one is supplied by \textup{(U4)}.
 224:   These are exactly the displayed matrix equations. The determinant
 225:   is \(\det C=(-1)^{d-1}a_0\). Multiplicativity of determinants gives
 226:   the assertion for blocks.
 227: \end{proof}
 228: 
 229: The coefficient \(a_0\) belongs to the displayed recurrence witness,
 230: not intrinsically to \((U,A,\mathcal R_U)\); see
 231: \Cref{prop:recurrence-witness-separation} for the resulting strict
 232: separation.
 233: 
 234: We now use the standard fan-out convention: a multiple context-free
 235: language (MCFL) is a \(k\)-MCFL for some finite \(k\ge1\). An infinite
 236: language is \emph{MCF-immune} if it contains no infinite MCFL
 237: sublanguage. The weak pumping lemma of Seki--Matsumura--Fujii--Kasami
 238: states that an infinite \(k\)-MCFL contains one word that can be pumped
 239: synchronously at \(2k\) factors \cite[Lemma~3.2]{SekiEtAl1991}. The
 240:   lemma makes no assertion about every sufficiently long word; that
 241:   stronger form fails for general MCFLs
 242:   \cite{KanazawaKobeleMichaelisSalvatiYoshinaka2014}.
 243: 
 244:   Duncan--Elder--Frenkel--Lyu's substitution lemma
 245:   \cite[Theorem~4.5]{DuncanElderFrenkelLiu2026} is stronger in a different
 246:   quantifier direction: after enough positions of each sufficiently long
 247:   word are marked, it gives either a bounded pump or a factorization in
 248:   which a tuple can be replaced by another tuple from one of finitely many
 249:   switchable families. It does not select a single family parameterized by
 250:   one exponent \(t\), nor does it make all replacement blocks simultaneous
 251:   powers of fixed words. Consequently it supplies neither a fixed affine
 252:   matrix-power expression nor a return time valid for every parameter on
 253:   one orbit. The switchable alternative also prevents it from replacing the
 254:   paired Ogden pump in the Zeckendorf argument above. Thus it does not by
 255:   itself imply any immunity result proved here. We use Seki et al.'s weaker
 256:   conclusion precisely because its one synchronized family has the fixed
 257:   \(2k\)-block power form needed below. The imported language statement is
 258:   only that family; its placement beyond the recurrence transient, the
 259:   finite-group return, and all arithmetic consequences are proved here.
 260: 
 261: \begin{theorem}[Synchronized local-congruence orbit]
 262:   \label{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}
 263:   Let \((U,A,\mathcal R_U)\) satisfy \textup{(U1)--(U4)}, and let
 264:   \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) be an infinite \(k\)-MCFL. There are words
 265:   \[
 266:     \begin{gathered}
 267:       a,u_1,\ldots,u_{k+1},v_1,\ldots,v_k\in A^\ast,\\
 268:       w_1,\ldots,w_k,s_1,\ldots,s_k\in A^\ast
 269:     \end{gathered}
 270:   \]
 271:   such that
 272:   \[
 273:     W(t)=a u_1v_1^tw_1s_1^tu_2\cdots
 274:           u_kv_k^tw_ks_k^tu_{k+1}\in L
 275:           \qquad(t\ge0),
 276:   \]
 277:   \[
 278:     \sum_{j=1}^k(\abs{v_j}+\abs{s_j})>0,
 279:   \]
 280:   and \(N(t):=\val_U(W(t))\) is strictly increasing. Moreover, for
 281:   every \(q\ge2\) with \(\gcd(q,a_0)=1\), there is \(H(q)\ge1\) such
 282:   that
 283:   \[
 284:     N(t+H(q))\equiv N(t)\pmod q
 285:     \qquad\text{for every }t\ge0.
 286:   \]
 287:   The factorization, and hence the orbit \(W(t)\), is independent of
 288:   \(q\).
 289: \end{theorem}
 290: 
 291: \begin{proof}
 292:   Delete the finitely many words of length less than \(J\). Since the
 293:   alphabet is finite, some \(a\in A^J\) is the prefix of infinitely many
 294:   remaining words; when \(J=0\), take \(a=\varepsilon\). For completeness,
 295:   fixed left quotient preserves fan-out. Introduce a fresh letter \(\#\),
 296:   let \(h(\#)=a\) and \(h(x)=x\) for \(x\in A\), intersect
 297:   \(h^{-1}(L)\) with the regular language \(\#A^*\), and erase \(\#\).
 298:   Inverse homomorphism, regular intersection, and homomorphism preserve
 299:   \(k\)-MCFLs without increasing the maximum nonterminal dimension
 300:   \cite[Proposition~2.3]{DuncanElderFrenkelLiu2026}. Hence
 301:   \[
 302:     L_a=\{x\in A^\ast:ax\in L\}
 303:   \]
 304:   is an infinite \(k\)-MCFL. The weak pumping lemma supplies
 305:   \(u_j,v_j,w_j,s_j\) with the displayed synchronized pumping property
 306:   and with at least one \(v_j\) or \(s_j\) nonempty.
 307: 
 308:   All digit actions after \(a\) are in the stationary range of
 309:   \textup{(U4)}. Fix an allowed modulus \(q\). By
 310:   \Cref{lem:affine-recurrence-block-action}, every nonempty matrix among
 311:   \[
 312:     M_{v_1},M_{s_1},\ldots,M_{v_k},M_{s_k}
 313:   \]
 314:   lies in the finite group
 315:   \(\operatorname{GL}_{d+1}(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\). One may take
 316:   \[
 317:     H(q)=\abs{\operatorname{GL}_{d+1}(\ZZ/q\ZZ)}
 318:       \le q^{(d+1)^2};
 319:   \]
 320:   Lagrange's theorem makes this a common multiple of their orders, and
 321:   empty blocks contribute the identity.
 322:   Replacing \(t\) by \(t+H(q)\) in the matrix product for \(W(t)\)
 323:   therefore leaves every powered block unchanged modulo \(q\), proving
 324:   the congruence simultaneously for all \(t\ge0\).
 325: 
 326:   Finally,
 327:   \[
 328:     \abs{W(t+1)}-\abs{W(t)}
 329:       =\sum_{j=1}^k(\abs{v_j}+\abs{s_j})>0.
 330:   \]
 331:   Both words are canonical, so \textup{(U3)} gives
 332:   \(N(t+1)>N(t)\).
 333: \end{proof}
 334: 
 335: \begin{lemma}[Explicit grammar for a synchronized ray]
 336:   \label{lem:synchronized-ray-linear-mcfg}
 337:   Fix \(k\ge1\), a word \(a\), and words
 338:   \(u_1,\ldots,u_{k+1},v_1,\ldots,v_k,w_1,\ldots,w_k,
 339:   s_1,\ldots,s_k\) over a finite alphabet. If
 340:   \[
 341:     \sum_{j=1}^k(\abs{v_j}+\abs{s_j})>0,
 342:   \]
 343:   then
 344:   \[
 345:     \left\{a u_1v_1^tw_1s_1^tu_2\cdots
 346:       u_kv_k^tw_ks_k^tu_{k+1}:t\ge0\right\}
 347:   \]
 348:   is an infinite linear \(2k\)-MCFL.
 349: \end{lemma}
 350: 
 351: \begin{proof}
 352:   Take a nonterminal \(X\) of fan-out \(2k\) and a start symbol \(T\) of
 353:   fan-out one. The productions are
 354:   \[
 355:     X(\varepsilon,\ldots,\varepsilon)\leftarrow,
 356:   \]
 357:   \[
 358:     X(v_1x_1,s_1x_2,\ldots,v_kx_{2k-1},s_kx_{2k})
 359:       \leftarrow X(x_1,\ldots,x_{2k}),
 360:   \]
 361:   and
 362:   \[
 363:     T(a u_1x_1w_1x_2u_2\cdots
 364:       u_kx_{2k-1}w_kx_{2k}u_{k+1})
 365:       \leftarrow X(x_1,\ldots,x_{2k}).
 366:   \]
 367:   After \(t\) recursive applications, the tuple derived from \(X\) is
 368:   \((v_1^t,s_1^t,\ldots,v_k^t,s_k^t)\), so the generated language is
 369:   exactly the displayed ray. Every variable occurs exactly once in each
 370:   production. The positive total pumped length makes the words at
 371:   distinct parameters have distinct lengths.
 372: \end{proof}
 373: 
 374: Put \(S=\{p:p\text{ is prime and }p\mid a_0\}\). On
 375: \(\NN_{\ge1}\), let \(\tau_S\) be the topology generated by the basis
 376: \[
 377:   \{(n+q\ZZ)\cap\NN_{\ge1}:n\ge1,\ q\ge1,\ \gcd(q,a_0)=1\}.
 378: \]
 379: Its completion is
 380: \[

 succeeded in 257ms:
 380: \[
 381:   \widehat{\ZZ}_{S^c}
 382:     :=\varprojlim_{\gcd(q,a_0)=1}\ZZ/q\ZZ
 383:     \cong\prod_{p\notin S}\ZZ_p.
 384: \]
 385: These are the adic topologies and completions studied by Broughan
 386: \cite{Broughan2003}. His Theorem~2.4 and Corollary~3.1 supply the
 387: \(\mathbb Q\)-space and Cantor-completion classifications used below. The
 388: new assertion here is that every synchronized MCFL orbit satisfies their
 389: no-isolated-point hypothesis, and hence yields the scattered-set
 390: obstruction.
 391: 
 392: \begin{theorem}[Adic recurrence and scattered-set obstruction for synchronized MCFL orbits]
 393:   \label{thm:mcfl-local-adic-fingerprint}
 394:   For the orbit \(Y=\{N(t):t\ge0\}\) supplied by
 395:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}, the following hold.
 396:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 397:     \item The subspace \(Y\subseteq(\NN_{\ge1},\tau_S)\) has no isolated
 398:     points and is homeomorphic to \(\mathbb Q\).
 399:     \item Its closure in \(\widehat{\ZZ}_{S^c}\) is homeomorphic to the
 400:     Cantor set.
 401:     \item If \(B\subseteq\NN_{\ge1}\) is scattered in \(\tau_S\), then
 402:     \[
 403:       \operatorname{Rep}_U(B)
 404:         :=\{w\in\mathcal R_U:\val_U(w)\in B\}
 405:     \]
 406:     is MCF-immune whenever it is infinite.
 407:   \end{enumerate}
 408: \end{theorem}
 409: 
 410: \begin{proof}
 411:   Let \(N(t)\in Y\), and let its basic neighborhood be specified modulo
 412:   an allowed \(q\). If \(q=1\), take any later orbit point. If \(q\ge2\),
 413:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit} gives
 414:   \(N(t+H(q))\equiv N(t)\pmod q\), and strict increase makes these points
 415:   distinct. Thus \(Y\) has no isolated point.
 416: 
 417:   The allowed congruences separate distinct positive integers: for
 418:   \(m\ne n\), choose a prime outside the finite set \(S\) that does not
 419:   divide \(m-n\). Consequently \(\tau_S\) is Hausdorff and metrizable;
 420:   it also has a countable basis. Hence \(Y\) is a countable metrizable
 421:   space without isolated points. The Sierpi\'nski characterization used
 422:   in \cite[Theorem~2.4]{Broughan2003} yields \(Y\cong\mathbb Q\).
 423: 
 424:   The profinite ring \(\widehat{\ZZ}_{S^c}\) is compact, metrizable, and
 425:   zero-dimensional. Its closed subspace \(\overline Y\) has no isolated
 426:   point: an isolated point of the closure would, by density of \(Y\),
 427:   belong to and be isolated in \(Y\). Thus \(\overline Y\) is a nonempty
 428:   compact perfect zero-dimensional metrizable space, and the classical
 429:   Cantor-space characterization gives the second assertion; compare
 430:   \cite[Corollary~3.1]{Broughan2003}.
 431: 
 432:   For the last assertion, an infinite MCFL sublanguage of
 433:   \(\operatorname{Rep}_U(B)\) would, by the first assertion, provide a
 434:   nonempty subspace \(Y\subseteq B\) without isolated points. This
 435:   contradicts scatteredness, which is hereditary to subspaces.
 436: \end{proof}
 437: 
 438: For a prime \(p\), write \(v_p(n)\) for the exponent of \(p\) in \(n\),
 439: and let \(\omega_{S^c}(n)=\#\{p\notin S:p\mid n\}\).
 440: For \(K\ge0\) and \(E=(E_p)_{p\in S}\in\NN_0^S\), define
 441: \[
 442:   X_{K,E}=\{n\ge1:\omega_{S^c}(n)\le K,
 443:                \ v_p(n)\le E_p\ (p\in S)\}.
 444: \]
 445: For \(X\subseteq\NN_{\ge1}\), let \(D_SX\) be the set of points of \(X\)
 446: that are not isolated in its \(\tau_S\)-subspace topology. Thus \(D_S\)
 447: is the intrinsic Cantor--Bendixson derivative. We use the height convention
 448: \[
 449:   \operatorname{ht}(X)=\min\{\alpha:D_S^\alpha X=\varnothing\}.
 450: \]
 451: 
 452: \begin{proposition}[Exact local prime-factor rank]
 453:   \label{prop:local-prime-factor-cb-rank}
 454:   For every \(K\ge1\),
 455:   \[
 456:     D_SX_{K,E}=X_{K-1,E}.
 457:   \]
 458:   Consequently
 459:   \[
 460:     D_S^{\,j}X_{K,E}=X_{K-j,E}\quad(0\le j\le K),
 461:     \qquad D_S^{\,K+1}X_{K,E}=\varnothing,
 462:   \]
 463:   and the Cantor--Bendixson height of \(X_{K,E}\) is exactly \(K+1\).
 464:   For \(K=0\), the nonempty set \(X_{0,E}\) is finite,
 465:   \(D_SX_{0,E}=\varnothing\), and its height is \(1\).
 466: \end{proposition}
 467: 
 468: \begin{proof}
 469:   Suppose first that \(n\in X_{K-1,E}\), and let \(q\ge1\) be coprime
 470:   to \(a_0\). Dirichlet's theorem supplies a prime
 471:   \(r\equiv1\pmod q\) outside the finite set \(S\cup\{p:p\mid n\}\).
 472:   Then \(nr\ne n\), \(nr\equiv n\pmod q\), the \(S\)-adic valuations
 473:   are unchanged, and
 474:   \[
 475:     \omega_{S^c}(nr)=\omega_{S^c}(n)+1\le K.
 476:   \]
 477:   Hence \(n\in D_SX_{K,E}\).
 478: 
 479:   Conversely, let \(n\in X_{K,E}\) have
 480:   \(\omega_{S^c}(n)=K\), and write
 481:   \[
 482:     n=c\prod_{i=1}^K\ell_i^{e_i},
 483:     \qquad c=\prod_{p\in S}p^{f_p},\quad 0\le f_p\le E_p,
 484:   \]
 485:   with distinct \(\ell_i\notin S\). Put
 486:   \(q_0=\prod_i\ell_i^{e_i+1}\). If \(m\in X_{K,E}\) and
 487:   \(m\equiv n\pmod {q_0}\), then
 488:   \(v_{\ell_i}(m)=e_i\) for every \(i\). The budget of \(K\) primes
 489:   outside \(S\) leaves no further such prime, so \(m\) belongs to the
 490:   finite set
 491:   \[
 492:     F=\left\{
 493:       \left(\prod_{p\in S}p^{g_p}\right)
 494:       \prod_{i=1}^K\ell_i^{e_i}:
 495:       0\le g_p\le E_p
 496:     \right\}.
 497:   \]
 498:   Choose a prime \(r\notin S\cup\{\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_K\}\) dividing
 499:   none of the finitely many nonzero differences \(m-n\), \(m\in F\).
 500:   Then \(q=q_0r\) is coprime to \(a_0\), and the \(q\)-neighborhood of
 501:   \(n\) meets \(X_{K,E}\) only at \(n\). Thus precisely the points with
 502:   fewer than \(K\) outside primes survive one derivative. Iteration
 503:   proves the formula. Finally \(X_{0,E}\) is a nonempty finite set (it
 504:   contains \(1\)), so the height is exactly \(K+1\).
 505: \end{proof}
 506: 
 507: Broughan's related full-adic result counts prime factors with
 508: multiplicity and identifies closures of the corresponding strata
 509: \cite[Theorem~4.3]{Broughan2003}. The preceding proposition is a local
 510: refinement of that analysis, not a new classification of the ambient adic
 511: space: it uses distinct prime support, deletes the finite set \(S\), imposes
 512: bounded \(S\)-adic valuations, and computes derivatives intrinsically in
 513: the indicated subspace rather than only in its ambient closure.
 514: 
 515: \begin{theorem}[Nonunit escape dichotomy and MCF-immunity]
 516:   \label{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}
 517:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) be an infinite MCFL. Then either
 518:   \[
 519:     \sup_{w\in L}\omega_{S^c}(\val_U(w))=\infty,
 520:   \]
 521:   or there is a prime \(p\mid a_0\) such that
 522:   \[
 523:     \sup_{w\in L}v_p(\val_U(w))=\infty.
 524:   \]
 525:   Consequently the following languages are MCF-immune whenever they are
 526:   infinite:
 527:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 528:     \item the prime representation language \(\mathcal P_U\);
 529:     \item every language on which \(\Omega(\val_U(w))\) is bounded;
 530:     \item every finite-prime-support language on which all
 531:     \(v_p(\val_U(w))\), \(p\mid a_0\), are bounded.
 532:   \end{enumerate}
 533:   In a unit system, every bounded-\(\omega(\val_U(w))\) language is
 534:   MCF-immune.
 535: \end{theorem}
 536: 
 537: \begin{proof}
 538:   If neither alternative held, finiteness of \(S\) would give
 539:   \(K\ge0\) and \(E\in\NN_0^S\) such that
 540:   \[
 541:     \{\val_U(w):w\in L\}\subseteq X_{K,E}.
 542:   \]
 543:   By \Cref{prop:local-prime-factor-cb-rank}, \(X_{K,E}\) is scattered.
 544:   This contradicts
 545:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-local-adic-fingerprint}. Prime values satisfy
 546:   \(\omega_{S^c}\le1\) and \(v_p\le1\); bounded \(\Omega\) bounds both
 547:   alternatives; and finite prime support bounds \(\omega_{S^c}\), with
 548:   the remaining valuations bounded by hypothesis. If \(S=\varnothing\),
 549:   the second alternative is absent, giving the unit-system assertion.
 550: \end{proof}
 551: 
 552: The second alternative is necessary. In ordinary base \(b\), where
 553: \(a_0=b\), the regular LSD-first language
 554: \(\{0^n1:n\ge0\}\) represents \(b^n\). Its distinct prime support is
 555: fixed, while \(v_p(b^n)\) is unbounded for every \(p\mid b\).
 556: 
 557: \begin{proposition}[Recurrence-witness inflation and strict separation]
 558:   \label{prop:recurrence-witness-separation}
 559:   If a tail has minimal recurrence polynomial \(\mu_U\), then for every
 560:   prime \(p\) it also has a recurrence witness with characteristic
 561:   polynomial \((X-p)\mu_U(X)\).  When \(\abs{\mu_U(0)}=1\), the trailing
 562:   coefficient of this witness has absolute value \(p\).
 563: 
 564:   Moreover, there are two canonical eventually recurrent
 565:   numeration systems with regular representation languages and recurrence
 566:   witnesses whose trailing-coefficient prime set is \(\{p\}\), but with
 567:   opposite bounded-outside-prime escape behavior:
 568:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 569:     \item ordinary base \(p\) contains the infinite regular language
 570:     \(\{0^n1:n\ge0\}\), whose values are \(p^n\);
 571:     \item the Zeckendorf system, with \(F_0=0,F_1=1\) and
 572:     \(F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_n\), equipped with the order-three recurrence
 573:     witness
 574:     \[
 575:       F_{n+3}=-pF_n+(1-p)F_{n+1}+(p+1)F_{n+2},
 576:     \]
 577:     contains no infinite MCFL \(L\) for which
 578:     \[
 579:       \sup_{w\in L}\omega_{\{p\}^c}(\val(w))<\infty.
 580:     \]
 581:   \end{enumerate}
 582:   Thus the prime divisors of an arbitrarily selected trailing coefficient
 583:   do not determine the escape behavior of the numeration system.
 584: \end{proposition}
 585: 
 586: \begin{proof}
 587:   The polynomial identity \(\mu_U(C)z_J=0\) remains valid after
 588:   multiplication by \(X-p\).  In the companion convention, the trailing
 589:   recurrence coefficient is the negative of the constant term of the
 590:   characteristic polynomial, proving the first assertion.
 591: 
 592:   The base-\(p\) assertion is the preceding example.  For the Zeckendorf
 593:   sequence, multiplication of its minimal polynomial by \(X-p\) gives
 594:   \[
 595:     (X-p)(X^2-X-1)
 596:       =X^3-(p+1)X^2+(p-1)X+p,
 597:   \]
 598:   which is exactly the displayed recurrence and has trailing coefficient
 599:   \(-p\).  This changes neither the sequence nor its regular canonical
 600:   representation language.  On the other hand, its minimal recurrence
 601:   has unit trailing coefficient.  If the displayed supremum were bounded
 602:   by \(K\), then
 603:   \(\omega(\val(w))\le K+1\) on \(L\), contrary to the unit-recurrence
 604:   conclusion of
 605:   \Cref{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}.  This proves the separation.
 606: \end{proof}
 607: 
 608: Let
 609: \[
 610:   \mu_U(X)=X^{d_{\mathrm r}}-c_{d_{\mathrm r}-1}X^{d_{\mathrm r}-1}
 611:     -\cdots-c_1X-c_0
 612: \]
 613: be the minimal recurrence polynomial of a tail of \(U\). Since it
 614: divides the characteristic polynomial of a recurrence allowed in
 615: \textup{(U4)}, its constant term is nonzero. Enlarge the recurrence
 616: threshold to \(J_{\mathrm r}\) so that this minimal recurrence holds for
 617: every \(n\ge J_{\mathrm r}\), and put
 618: \[
 619:   S_{\mathrm r}=\{p:p\text{ is prime and }p\mid c_0\}.
 620: \]
 621: 

 succeeded in 266ms:
   1: \begin{definition}[Geometric synchronized scheme]
   2:   \label{def:geometric-synchronized-scheme}
   3:   A geometric synchronized scheme for \(U\) consists of
   4:   \(a\in A^{J_{\mathrm r}}\), an integer \(k\ge1\), integers
   5:   \(c\ge1,b\ge2\), and words
   6:   \[
   7:     u_1,\ldots,u_{k+1},\quad
   8:     v_1,\ldots,v_k,\quad w_1,\ldots,w_k,\quad
   9:     s_1,\ldots,s_k\in A^\ast,
  10:   \]
  11:   such that
  12:   \[
  13:     W(t)=a u_1v_1^tw_1s_1^tu_2\cdots
  14:       u_kv_k^tw_ks_k^tu_{k+1}\in\mathcal R_U
  15:       \qquad(t\ge0),
  16:   \]
  17:   \[
  18:     \sum_{j=1}^k(\abs{v_j}+\abs{s_j})>0,
  19:     \qquad \val_U(W(t))=cb^t\quad(t\ge0).
  20:   \]
  21: \end{definition}
