  23: \begin{lemma}[Arithmetic input of Evertse]
  24:   \label{lem:evertse-quotient-support-input}
  25:   Let \(K\) be a number field. For \(x\in K^\times\), let \(P_K(x)\)
  26:   be the largest norm of a finite prime ideal \(\mathfrak p\) for which
  27:   \(\operatorname{ord}_{\mathfrak p}(x)\ne0\), with \(P_K(x)=1\) when
  28:   \(x\) is a unit. If \(Z=(Z(n))_{n\ge0}\) is a nondegenerate linear
  29:   recurrence over \(K\) whose minimal shift-annihilating polynomial has
  30:   at least two distinct nonzero roots, then
  31:   \[
  32:     \lim_{\substack{r\to\infty\\ r>s\ge0,\ Z(s)\ne0}}
  33:       P_K\!\left(\frac{Z(r)}{Z(s)}\right)=\infty.
  34:   \]
  35:   In particular, a rational-integer recurrence with no zero terms, all
  36:   supported on one finite set of rational primes, cannot be both
  37:   nondegenerate and have at least two characteristic roots.
  38: \end{lemma}
  39: 
  40: \begin{proof}
  41:   The displayed uniform limit is exactly
  42:   \cite[Theorem~3]{Evertse1984}. For the consequence, let \(S\) be the
  43:   finite rational-prime support and choose a number field \(K\) containing
  44:   the characteristic roots and coefficients. If \(Z(r),Z(s)\ne0\), the
  45:   principal fractional ideal generated by \(Z(r)/Z(s)\) is supported
  46:   only at prime ideals above primes in \(S\). Every prime ideal
  47:   \(\mathfrak p\) above \(p\in S\) has
  48:   \[
  49:     \operatorname N\mathfrak p=p^{f(\mathfrak p/p)}
  50:       \le p^{[K:\mathbb Q]}.
  51:   \]
  52:   Consequently
  53:   \[
  54:     P_K\!\left(\frac{Z(r)}{Z(s)}\right)
  55:       \le \max\bigl(\{p^{[K:\mathbb Q]}:p\in S\}\cup\{1\}\bigr)
  56:   \]
  57:   for every \(r,s\) with nonzero denominator. This fixed bound
  58:   contradicts Evertse's limit when there are at least two characteristic
  59:   roots. Here and below, ``characteristic roots of a sequence'' always
  60:   means the roots of its minimal shift-annihilating polynomial, not roots
  61:   introduced by a nonminimal recurrence witness.
  62: \end{proof}
  63: 
  64: \begin{lemma}[Increasing \(S\)-unit recurrences]
  65:   \label{lem:s-unit-increasing-recurrence-rigidity}
  66:   Let \(Y=(Y(n))_{n\ge0}\) be a strictly increasing sequence of positive
  67:   rational integers satisfying a linear recurrence. If all prime
  68:   divisors of all \(Y(n)\) belong to one finite set, then there are
  69:   integers \(r\ge0,h\ge1,c\ge1\), and \(b\ge2\) such that
  70:   \[
  71:     Y(r+hn)=cb^n\qquad(n\ge0).
  72:   \]
  73: \end{lemma}
  74: 
  75: \begin{proof}
  76:   Write the standard exponential-polynomial expansion, after discarding
  77:   the contribution of the zero characteristic root and a finite initial
  78:   segment, as
  79:   \[
  80:     Y(n)=\sum_{i=1}^m f_i(n)\alpha_i^n,
  81:   \]
  82:   where the \(\alpha_i\) are distinct nonzero algebraic numbers and the
  83:   nonzero \(f_i\) are algebraic polynomials. Partition the roots by
  84:   \(\alpha_i\sim\alpha_j\) when \(\alpha_i/\alpha_j\) is a root of
  85:   unity. Choose \(h_0\ge1\) divisible by the orders of all such
  86:   quotients. For each \(j\in\{0,\ldots,h_0-1\}\), substitution of
  87:   \(n=j+h_0t\) and collection within the equivalence classes gives
  88:   \[
  89:     Y(j+h_0t)=\sum_C g_{C,j}(t)\beta_C^t,
  90:     \qquad \beta_C=\alpha_i^{h_0}\quad(i\in C).
  91:   \]
  92:   Delete the identically zero summands. Distinct remaining \(\beta_C\)
  93:   have quotient that is not a root of unity. Thus every residue
  94:   subsequence is a nonzero nondegenerate recurrence; it is nonzero
  95:   because all of its terms are positive.
  96: 
  97:   Every term of every residue subsequence is a nonzero rational integer
  98:   supported on the prescribed finite set. If a residue subsequence had
  99:   at least two characteristic roots, the consequence in
 100:   \Cref{lem:evertse-quotient-support-input} would contradict the fixed
 101:   support of all its term quotients. Hence every residue subsequence has
 102:   exactly one characteristic root and therefore has the form
 103:   \[
 104:     Z(n)=f(n)\lambda^n
 105:   \]
 106:   with one characteristic root.
 107: 
 108:   Choose one residue subsequence and shift its index if necessary so
 109:   that the displayed expansion holds from \(n=0\), and put
 110:   \(e=\deg f\). Let \(E\) denote the forward shift. Over
 111:   \(\overline{\mathbb Q}\), finite differences give
 112:   \[
 113:     (E-\lambda)^{e+1}Z=0,
 114:     \qquad (E-\lambda)^eZ\ne0,
 115:   \]
 116:   because the \(e\)-th difference of the nonzero degree-\(e\) polynomial
 117:   \(f\) is a nonzero constant. Hence the monic minimal shift-annihilator
 118:   over \(\overline{\mathbb Q}\) is exactly
 119:   \((X-\lambda)^{e+1}\), and the Hankel rank of \(Z\) over that field is
 120:   \(e+1\).
 121: 
 122:   The Hankel matrix has rational entries, and a rational matrix has the
 123:   same rank over \(\mathbb Q\) and over \(\overline{\mathbb Q}\).
 124:   Therefore a minimal dependence among its shift columns gives a monic
 125:   polynomial \(P\in\mathbb Q[X]\) of the same degree \(e+1\). After
 126:   extending scalars, minimality and equality of degrees force
 127:   \[
 128:     P(X)=(X-\lambda)^{e+1}.
 129:   \]
 130:   For every
 131:   \(\sigma\in\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/\mathbb Q)\), the
 132:   polynomial \(P\) is fixed and has only the root \(\lambda\); thus
 133:   \(\sigma(\lambda)=\lambda\). Hence \(\lambda\in\mathbb Q\). Now
 134:   \(f(n)=Z(n)/\lambda^n\in\mathbb Q\)
 135:   for every \(n\), and interpolation from \(\deg(f)+1\) integer
 136:   arguments gives \(f\in\mathbb Q[X]\). If \(f\) were nonconstant, clear its
 137:   denominators to obtain a nonconstant \(g\in\mathbb Z[X]\). The
 138:   identity \(Z(n)=f(n)\lambda^n\) would confine every prime divisor of
 139:   every nonzero \(g(n)\) to a fixed finite set. Indeed, if
 140:   \(\lambda=a/b\) is in lowest terms and \(g=Df\), then
 141:   \[
 142:     g(n)a^n=Db^nZ(n).
 143:   \]
 144:   Thus that fixed set is the union of the prescribed prime set and the
 145:   prime divisors of \(Dab\). This contradicts Schur's theorem that a
 146:   nonconstant integer polynomial has values divisible by infinitely
 147:   many primes; see, for example, \cite{HardyWright2008}. Hence \(f\) is
 148:   constant.
 149: 
 150:   We have therefore \(Z(n)=c\lambda^n\), where
 151:   \(c=Z(0)\) is a positive integer. If \(\lambda=a/b\) in lowest terms,
 152:   integrality for every \(n\) forces \(b^n\mid c\) for every \(n\), so
 153:   \(b=1\). Positivity and strict increase then force
 154:   \(\lambda\in\mathbb Z_{\ge2}\). Translating the chosen residue and
 155:   index shift back to \(Y\) supplies the asserted \(r\) and \(h\).
 156: \end{proof}
 157: 
 158: \begin{theorem}[Geometric-ray characterization]
 159:   \label{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}
 160:   For a canonical eventually recurrent numeration system satisfying
 161:   \textup{(U1)--(U4)}, the following are equivalent.
 162:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 163:     \item There are a finite \(k\) and an infinite \(k\)-MCFL
 164:     \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) such that
 165:     \[
 166:       \sup_{w\in L}\omega_{S_{\mathrm r}^c}(\val_U(w))<\infty.
 167:     \]
 168:     \item The system admits a geometric synchronized scheme in the
 169:     sense of \Cref{def:geometric-synchronized-scheme}.
 170:     \item The language \(\mathcal R_U\) contains an infinite linear MCFL
 171:     \(L_0=\{W(t):t\ge0\}\) of finite fan-out for which there are
 172:     \(c\ge1,b\ge2\) satisfying
 173:     \[
 174:       \val_U(W(t))=cb^t\qquad(t\ge0).
 175:     \]
 176:   \end{enumerate}
 177: \end{theorem}
 178: 
 179: \begin{proof}
 180:   Suppose first that \textup{(i)} holds. Use the minimal tail recurrence
 181:   as the witness in
 182:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}, and write its resulting
 183:   ray and strictly increasing value sequence as \(W(t)\) and \(N(t)\).
 184:   Let
 185:   \[
 186:     K_0=\max_{t\ge0}\omega_{S_{\mathrm r}^c}(N(t)),
 187:   \]
 188:   and choose \(t_0\) attaining the maximum. Let \(T\) be the set of
 189:   primes outside \(S_{\mathrm r}\) that divide \(N(t_0)\), and put
 190:   \(q=\prod_{\ell\in T}\ell\). If \(T\ne\varnothing\), take the return
 191:   time \(H=H(q)\) from
 192:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}; if \(T=\varnothing\),
 193:   put \(H=1\). Every prime in \(T\) divides every \(N(t_0+nH)\). A prime
 194:   divisor outside \(S_{\mathrm r}\cup T\) would therefore give at least
 195:   \(K_0+1\) distinct prime divisors outside \(S_{\mathrm r}\), contrary
 196:   to the definition of \(K_0\). Thus all terms of
 197:   \[
 198:     Y(n)=N(t_0+nH)
 199:   \]
 200:   are supported on the fixed finite set \(S_{\mathrm r}\cup T\).
 201: 
 202:   To make the termwise-product closure explicit, put
 203:   \(n_0=d_{\mathrm r}+1\), list the \(2k\) powered affine blocks as
 204:   \(B_1,\ldots,B_{2k}\), and form
 205:   \[
 206:     K=B_1\otimes\cdots\otimes B_{2k}.
 207:   \]
 208:   Since \(K^t=B_1^t\otimes\cdots\otimes B_{2k}^t\), expansion of the
 209:   interspersed constant block matrices expresses \(N(t)\) as a fixed
 210:   linear functional of \(K^t\). Cayley--Hamilton therefore gives a
 211:   recurrence of order at most
 212:   \[
 213:     R=n_0^{2k}=(d_{\mathrm r}+1)^{2k}.
 214:   \]
 215:   Arithmetic subsequences of a recurrence are recurrences, so \(Y\) is a
 216:   strictly increasing positive integer recurrence. By
 217:   \Cref{lem:s-unit-increasing-recurrence-rigidity}, there are
 218:   \(r\ge0,h\ge1,c\ge1,b\ge2\) such that
 219:   \(N(t_\ast+h_\ast n)=cb^n\), where
 220:   \(t_\ast=t_0+Hr\) and \(h_\ast=Hh\).
 221: 
 222:   In the synchronized factorization, replace
 223:   \[
 224:     u_j\ \text{by}\ u_jv_j^{t_\ast},\qquad
 225:     v_j\ \text{by}\ v_j^{h_\ast},\qquad
 226:     w_j\ \text{by}\ w_js_j^{t_\ast},\qquad
 227:     s_j\ \text{by}\ s_j^{h_\ast}.
 228:   \]
 229:   This absorbs the fixed powers and parametrizes exactly the subray
 230:   \(W(t_\ast+h_\ast n)\). Its total pumped length remains positive, so
 231:   it is a geometric synchronized scheme. This proves
 232:   \textup{(i)}\(\Rightarrow\)\textup{(ii)}.
 233: 
 234:   The implication \textup{(ii)}\(\Rightarrow\)\textup{(iii)} is
 235:   \Cref{lem:synchronized-ray-linear-mcfg}. Finally, under
 236:   \textup{(iii)}, the language \(L_0\) is an infinite MCFL and
 237:   \[
 238:     \omega_{S_{\mathrm r}^c}(cb^t)\le\omega(cb)
 239:     \qquad(t\ge0),
 240:   \]
 241:   proving \textup{(i)}.
 242: \end{proof}
 243: 
 244: \begin{lemma}[Prime support of the geometric ratio]
 245:   \label{lem:geometric-ratio-tail-support}
 246:   In every geometric synchronized scheme
 247:   \(\val_U(W(t))=cb^t\), each prime divisor of \(b\) divides the
 248:   constant coefficient \(c_0\) of the minimal tail recurrence.  Equivalently,
 249:   \[
 250:     \operatorname{rad}(b)\mid\operatorname{rad}(\abs{c_0}).
 251:   \]
 252: \end{lemma}
 253: 
 254: \begin{proof}
 255:   Suppose that a prime \(p\mid b\) does not divide \(c_0\), put
 256:   \(e=v_p(c)\), and take the allowed modulus \(q=p^{e+1}\).  Every
 257:   nonempty block matrix among the \(M_{v_j}\) and \(M_{s_j}\) is invertible
 258:   modulo \(q\) by \Cref{lem:affine-recurrence-block-action}.  A common
 259:   multiple \(H\ge1\) of their orders gives, by the matrix product defining
 260:   the scheme exactly as in the proof of
 261:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit},
 262:   \[
 263:     \val_U(W(t+H))\equiv\val_U(W(t))\pmod q
 264:     \qquad(t\ge0).
 265:   \]
 266:   At \(t=0\) this says
 267:   \[
 268:     cb^H\equiv c\pmod {p^{e+1}}.
 269:   \]
 270:   But \(p\mid b\) implies \(p\nmid b^H-1\), so
 271:   \(v_p(c(b^H-1))=e\), contradicting the displayed congruence.
 272: \end{proof}
 273: 
 274: \begin{corollary}[Positive semidecidability on promised presentations]
 275:   \label{cor:bounded-outside-support-semidecidable}
 276:   On the promised class of valid presentations satisfying
 277:   \textup{(U1)--(U4)}, suppose the finite digit alphabet, recurrence and
 278:   initial data, and a DFA recognizing \(\mathcal R_U\) are supplied,
 279:   together with a supplied or effectively certified minimal tail
 280:   recurrence and a valid threshold \(J_{\mathrm r}\). Then the set of
 281:   positive promised instances of the property in
 282:   \Cref{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}\textup{(i)} is
 283:   recursively enumerable, equivalently it has a \(\Sigma^0_1\) upper
 284:   bound. No recognition procedure for membership in the promised input
 285:   class is asserted.
 286: \end{corollary}
 287: 
 288: \begin{proof}
 289:   Enumerate \(k\ge1\), \(a\in A^{J_{\mathrm r}}\), and all finite tuples
 290:   of words occurring in
 291:   \Cref{def:geometric-synchronized-scheme}, rejecting tuples of zero
 292:   total pumped length. For a fixed tuple, whether the DFA accepts
 293:   \(W(t)\) for every \(t\ge0\) is decidable: the tuple of powers of the
 294:   finitely many pumped-block transformations is eventually periodic in
 295:   the finite transition monoid, and its preperiod and period are found
 296:   by exhaustive iteration.
 297: 
 298:   The affine matrices construct an exact linear recurrence for
 299:   \[
 300:     N(t)=\val_U(W(t)).
 301:   \]
 302:   With \(d_{\mathrm r}\) the minimal tail order, the Kronecker construction
 303:   in the proof of
 304:   \Cref{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray} has dimension
 305:   \[
 306:     R=(d_{\mathrm r}+1)^{2k};
 307:   \]
 308:   it is computable from the candidate and is an upper bound for the
 309:   recurrence order of \(N(t)\). For a tuple accepted at every parameter,
 310:   compute \(c=N(0)\). The only
 311:   possible integer ratio is \(b=N(1)/N(0)\). Check that
 312:   \(b\in\mathbb Z_{\ge2}\), and then decide whether the recurrence
 313:   \(N(t+1)-bN(t)\) is identically zero by checking its first \(R\) terms;
 314:   Cayley--Hamilton makes those \(R\) zero values sufficient. Thus each
 315:   candidate scheme is decidable, and the enumeration halts exactly on
 316:   positive promised instances by
 317:   \Cref{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}. No claim of a
 318:   decision procedure for negative instances is made.
 319: \end{proof}
 320: 
 321: \begin{remark}[Exact algorithmic boundary]
 322:   \label{rem:general-decision-interface}
 323:   The preceding corollary does not yield a decision procedure. To turn
 324:   its enumeration into one, it would suffice to compute from the input
 325:   DFA and minimal tail recurrence a bound \(B\) such that every positive
 326:   instance has a geometric synchronized scheme with fan-out and total
 327:   constant and pumped-block length at most \(B\). No such compression
 328:   follows from DFA pumping: replacing a long loop by a shorter word with
 329:   the same DFA endpoints need not preserve its affine recurrence matrix,
 330:   and can therefore destroy the identity
 331:   \(\val_U(W(t+1))=b\val_U(W(t))\).
 332: 
 333:   The neighboring decision theory does not close this step. Skolem asks
 334:   whether one term of a recurrence vanishes, while Positivity and Ultimate
 335:   Positivity ask for signs of all or all sufficiently late terms; these
 336:   problems remain open in unrestricted order
 337:   \cite{OuaknineWorrell2012,AkshayBazilleGenestVahanwala2024}.
 338:   Tarasov--Vyalyi relate an orbit-hitting problem to regular-language
 339:   intersection \cite{TarasovVyalyi2011}, while unrestricted multiplicative
 340:   matrix equations include undecidable families
 341:   \cite{BellEtAl2008MatrixEquations}. Our candidate check instead asks
 342:   whether the explicitly constructed recurrence
 343:   \(N(t+1)-bN(t)\) is identically zero; no reduction in either direction is
 344:   claimed. None of these observations is a reduction for the companion-form
 345:   digit matrices here, and conditions
 346:   \textup{(U1)--(U3)} must also be preserved. Thus general decidability,
 347:   undecidability, and any hardness classification remain open; the proved
 348:   effective statement is exactly the positive semidecision above. The
 349:   Perron-recurrence subclass of greedy linear numeration bases is resolved
 350:   separately below.
 351: \end{remark}
 352: 
 353: \begin{theorem}[Arbitrarily deep quotient congruences]
 354:   \label{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain}
 355:   Suppose \(\abs{a_0}=1\), and let \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) be an
 356:   infinite MCFL. For arbitrary sequences \(M_i\ge1\) and \(r_i\ge1\),
 357:   \(L\) contains a strictly increasing sequence \(N_i\) such that
 358:   \[
 359:     N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i>1,\qquad
 360:     Q_i\equiv1\pmod {M_iN_i^{r_i}}.

 succeeded in 255ms:
 361:   \]
 362:   If \(M_i\) is divisible by every prime at most \(B_i\), then every
 363:   prime divisor of \(Q_i\) exceeds \(B_i\).
 364: \end{theorem}
 365: 
 366: \begin{proof}
 367:   Take the fixed orbit \(N(t)\) from
 368:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}, discarding finitely many
 369:   initial parameters so that \(N(t)\ge2\). Having selected
 370:   \(N_i=N(t_i)\), apply the synchronized congruence with
 371:   \[
 372:     q_i=M_iN_i^{r_i+1}.
 373:   \]
 374:   Set \(t_{i+1}=t_i+H(q_i)\). Strict increase and the congruence give
 375:   \[
 376:     N_{i+1}=N_i+c_iM_iN_i^{r_i+1}
 377:            =N_i(1+c_iM_iN_i^{r_i})
 378:   \]
 379:   for an integer \(c_i\ge1\). This proves the first assertion. Under
 380:   the last hypothesis, \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod p\) for every prime
 381:   \(p\le B_i\), so none of those primes divides \(Q_i\).
 382: \end{proof}
 383: 
 384: \begin{theorem}[Induced full divisibility tree]
 385:   \label{thm:unit-mcfl-divisibility-tree}
 386:   Under the hypotheses of
 387:   \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain}, there is an injection
 388:   \[
 389:     \Phi:\NN^{<\omega}\longrightarrow L
 390:   \]
 391:   such that
 392:   \[
 393:     \val_U(\Phi(\sigma))\mid\val_U(\Phi(\tau))
 394:       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \sigma\preceq\tau,
 395:   \]
 396:   where \(\preceq\) denotes the prefix order. The quotients attached to
 397:   distinct edges can be chosen pairwise coprime. Moreover, given an
 398:   arbitrary prescribed threshold for each edge, every prime divisor of
 399:   its quotient can be required to exceed that threshold.
 400: \end{theorem}
 401: 
 402: \begin{proof}
 403:   Use the fixed strictly increasing orbit from
 404:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}. Here is an explicit
 405:   parent-before-child enumeration. If \(p_i\) is the \(i\)-th prime, encode
 406:   \(\sigma=(\sigma_0,\ldots,\sigma_{m-1})\) by
 407:   \[
 408:     \kappa(\sigma)=2^m\prod_{i=0}^{m-1}p_{i+2}^{\sigma_i+1}.
 409:   \]
 410:   Unique factorization makes \(\kappa\) injective, and
 411:   \(\kappa(\sigma^\frown j)>\kappa(\sigma)\). Enumerating the nonroot
 412:   nodes, equivalently their incoming edges, by increasing \(\kappa\)
 413:   therefore has order type \(\omega\) and places every parent before each
 414:   child. Map
 415:   the root to any orbit word of value \(R_0\ge2\). Suppose finitely many
 416:   edges have been assigned, and consider a new edge from a parent of
 417:   value \(P\). Let \(R\) be divisible by \(R_0\), by every previously
 418:   assigned edge quotient, and by every prime not exceeding the prescribed
 419:   threshold for the new edge. Apply the synchronized orbit congruence
 420:   modulo \(P^2R\) at the parameter of the parent. A sufficiently large
 421:   positive multiple of the return time gives an unused orbit word of
 422:   value \(P'>P\) with
 423:   \[
 424:     P'\equiv P\pmod {P^2R}.
 425:   \]
 426:   Hence \(P'=PQ\), where \(Q>1\) and \(Q\equiv1\pmod {PR}\). In
 427:   particular, \(Q\) is coprime to the root and to every earlier edge
 428:   quotient, and no prime up to the prescribed threshold divides \(Q\).
 429: 
 430:   Recursion defines \(\Phi\) on every node. The value at a node is the
 431:   root value times the product of the edge quotients along its path.
 432:   Since the root and all distinct edge quotients are pairwise coprime,
 433:   the value at \(\sigma\) divides the value at \(\tau\) exactly when the
 434:   root-to-\(\sigma\) edge set is contained in the root-to-\(\tau\) edge
 435:   set. For two nodes of a rooted tree, containment of their root-path edge
 436:   sets holds exactly when the first node is an ancestor of the second;
 437:   here this is precisely \(\sigma\preceq\tau\). This also proves injectivity.
 438: \end{proof}
 439: 
 440: \begin{theorem}[Prime immunity for recurrent numeration systems]
 441:   \label{thm:recurrent-numeration-prime-cf-immunity}
 442:   Let \((U,A,\mathcal R_U)\) satisfy \textup{(U1)--(U4)}, and put
 443:   \[
 444:     \mathcal P_U
 445:       :=\{w\in\mathcal R_U:\val_U(w)\text{ is prime}\}.
 446:   \]
 447:   Then \(\mathcal P_U\) is MCF-immune, and therefore CF-immune,
 448:   REG-immune, and not recognizable by a finite automaton over \(A\).
 449: 
 450:   If, in addition, \(\abs{a_0}=1\), then every infinite multiple
 451:   context-free
 452:   \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) has unbounded
 453:   \(\omega(\val_U(w))\). More precisely, \(L\) contains a
 454:   coprime-quotient chain \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\) with
 455:   \(Q_i>1\) and \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\). Thus the bounded-\(\omega\),
 456:   bounded-\(\Omega\), and finite-prime-support languages of a unit
 457:   system are MCF-immune whenever they are infinite. Without the unit
 458:   hypothesis, every bounded-\(\Omega\) language is still MCF-immune.
 459: \end{theorem}
 460: 
 461: \begin{proof}
 462:   The MCF-immunity assertions follow from
 463:   \Cref{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}, including its
 464:   bounded-\(\Omega\) conclusion. In a unit system \(S\) is empty, so
 465:   that theorem gives unbounded \(\omega\). Taking
 466:   \(M_i=r_i=1\) in
 467:   \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain} gives the stated chain.
 468:   Regular languages are context-free and context-free languages are
 469:   MCFLs. Finally, \textup{(U2)} and Euclid's theorem make
 470:   \(\mathcal P_U\) infinite.
 471: \end{proof}
 472: 
 473: \begin{corollary}[Linear numeration bases with Perron recurrence]
 474:   \label{cor:linear-perron-prime-cf-immunity}
 475:   Let \(U=(U_n)_{n\ge0}\) be a standard greedy linear numeration basis in
 476:   the following precise sense: \(U_0=1\), the \(U_n\) are strictly
 477:   increasing integers with bounded quotients, \(\mathcal R_U\) is the LSD-first
 478:   reversal of the usual greedy representation language, and \(U\)
 479:   has minimal tail recurrence polynomial equal to the minimal polynomial
 480:   of a Perron number \(\beta\). Thus
 481:   \(\beta>1\) is a real algebraic integer and every other conjugate
 482:   \(\beta'\) satisfies \(\abs{\beta'}<\beta\); compare
 483:   \cite{LindMarcus1995,Rigo2014Vol2}. Then its prime
 484:   representation language is MCF-immune, hence CF-immune, REG-immune and
 485:   nonrecognizable. The same statements hold in the usual MSD-first
 486:   convention.
 487: 
 488:   If the dominant Perron number is a unit and the chosen recurrence has
 489:   trailing coefficient \(a_0=\pm1\), then the full coprime-quotient and
 490:   bounded-prime-support MCF-immunity conclusions of
 491:   \Cref{thm:recurrent-numeration-prime-cf-immunity} hold.
 492: \end{corollary}
 493: 
 494: \begin{proof}
 495:   Greedy expansion gives a unique representation of every positive
 496:   integer, and its length-\(m\) words represent values between successive
 497:   place-value thresholds; thus \textup{(U1)--(U3)} hold. The defining
 498:   Perron recurrence gives \textup{(U4)}. Apply
 499:   \Cref{thm:recurrent-numeration-prime-cf-immunity}. The immunity proof
 500:   uses only \textup{(U1)--(U4)} and does not assume that the full greedy
 501:   language is regular. In the Pisot subclass, Frougny's finite
 502:   normalization theorem supplies a finite transducer, and the associated
 503:   canonical syntax and Bertrand/substitution descriptions are standard
 504:   \cite{Frougny1992,BruyereHansel1997,Fabre1995,FrougnySolomyak1992}.
 505:   Finally, multiple context-free, context-free, and
 506:   regular languages are closed under reversal, so changing between the LSD-first proof
 507:   convention and the usual MSD-first convention preserves every
 508:   conclusion.
 509: \end{proof}
 510: 
 511: \begin{example}[Pisot and non-Pisot Perron systems]
 512:   \label{ex:pisot-unit-nonunit}
 513:   The Fibonacci recurrence has characteristic polynomial
 514:   \(X^2-X-1\), the Pell recurrence has \(X^2-2X-1\), and the
 515:   Tribonacci recurrence has \(X^3-X^2-X-1\). Their trailing recurrence
 516:   coefficient is \(a_0=1\), so the iterated conclusion applies. In
 517:   ordinary base \(b\), \(U_n=b^n\) and \(a_0=b\); prime MCF-immunity
 518:   follows, strengthening the classical fixed-base CF-immunity conclusion
 519:   to MCF-immunity, but the unit iteration is not being asserted.
 520: 
 521:   A genuinely nonintegral, nonunit Pisot example is obtained from
 522:   \[
 523:     U_0=1,\qquad U_1=3,\qquad
 524:     U_{n+2}=2U_{n+1}+2U_n.
 525:   \]
 526:   If \(2<U_{n+1}/U_n\le3\), then
 527:   \[
 528:     \frac{U_{n+2}}{U_{n+1}}
 529:       =2+\frac{2U_n}{U_{n+1}}\in[8/3,3),
 530:   \]
 531:   so induction from \(U_1/U_0=3\) proves strict increase and bounded
 532:   quotients. The irreducible polynomial \(X^2-2X-2\) annihilates this
 533:   nonzero rational sequence, hence is its minimal tail polynomial. Its
 534:   dominant root is \(1+\sqrt3\), and its trailing coefficient is
 535:   \(a_0=2\), so the action is singular modulo \(2\). Nevertheless,
 536:   \Cref{cor:linear-perron-bounded-support-classification} shows that every
 537:   infinite MCFL in this nonintegral system acquires unbounded prime support
 538:   away from \(2\); singularity of the ambient action does not produce a
 539:   valuation-only escape language.
 540: 
 541:   The polynomial \(X^2-5X+5\) gives a genuinely non-Pisot instance. Its
 542:   dominant root \(\beta=(5+\sqrt5)/2\) is Perron, while its other conjugate
 543:   \((5-\sqrt5)/2\) is greater than one. The integer sequence
 544:   \[
 545:     U_0=1,\qquad U_1=4,\qquad
 546:     U_{n+2}=5U_{n+1}-5U_n
 547:   \]
 548:   begins \(1,4,15,55,200,\ldots\) and is strictly increasing. Indeed,
 549:   \[
 550:     U_{n+1}>3U_n>0
 551:   \]
 552:   holds at \(n=0\), and if it holds at \(n\), then
 553:   \[
 554:     U_{n+2}=5(U_{n+1}-U_n)
 555:       >\frac{10}{3}U_{n+1}>3U_{n+1}.
 556:   \]
 557:   Moreover, if \(3<U_{n+1}/U_n\le4\), then
 558:   \[
 559:     \frac{U_{n+2}}{U_{n+1}}
 560:       =5-\frac{5U_n}{U_{n+1}}
 561:       \le 5-\frac54<4,
 562:   \]
 563:   while the lower bound was just proved; starting from \(U_1/U_0=4\)
 564:   therefore gives \(3<U_{n+1}/U_n\le4\) for every \(n\). Thus the quotients
 565:   are bounded, so its greedy language is the greedy language of a linear
 566:   numeration basis with Perron recurrence in the stated sense. The
 567:   classification below implies that every infinite MCFL in this standard
 568:   greedy language has unbounded prime support away from \(5\).
 569: \end{example}
 570: 
 571: \begin{corollary}[Bounded support for Perron-recurrence numeration]
 572:   \label{cor:linear-perron-bounded-support-classification}
 573:   Let \(U\) be a greedy linear numeration basis in the precise sense of
 574:   \Cref{cor:linear-perron-prime-cf-immunity}, let \(\beta\) be its dominant
 575:   Perron number, and let \(S_{\mathrm r}\) be the prime divisors of the
 576:   constant coefficient of its minimal tail recurrence.  The following are
 577:   equivalent:
 578:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 579:     \item there is an infinite MCFL \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) such that
 580:     \[
 581:       \sup_{w\in L}\omega_{S_{\mathrm r}^c}(\val_U(w))<\infty;
 582:     \]
 583:     \item \(U\) admits a geometric synchronized scheme;
 584:     \item \(\beta\in\mathbb Z_{\ge2}\).
 585:   \end{enumerate}
 586:   Consequently, if \(\beta\) is nonintegral, every infinite MCFL
 587:   \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) has unbounded prime support outside
 588:   \(S_{\mathrm r}\).  For effective input as in
 589:   \Cref{cor:bounded-outside-support-semidecidable}, the three equivalent
 590:   properties are decidable in this Perron-recurrence class: the answer is positive
 591:   exactly when the supplied minimal polynomial has degree one.
 592: \end{corollary}
 593: 
 594: \begin{proof}
 595:   The equivalence of \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)} is
 596:   \Cref{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}.  Suppose that
 597:   \textup{(ii)} holds, write
 598:   \[
 599:     \val_U(W(t))=cb^t,
 600:     \qquad \abs{W(t)}=L+Dt,
 601:   \]
 602:   where \(D>0\) is the total pumped length.  In a greedy \(U\)-system, a
 603:   canonical word of length \(m\) represents a value in the half-open interval
 604:   \([U_{m-1},U_m)\).  Hence
 605:   \[
 606:     U_{L+Dt-1}\le cb^t<U_{L+Dt}.
 607:   \]
 608:   The standard solution of the recurrence defined by the minimal polynomial
 609:   of the Perron number gives
 610:   \[
 611:     \lim_{n\to\infty}U_n^{1/n}=\beta.
 612:   \]
 613:   Here is the required nonvanishing argument. Over the splitting field the
 614:   minimal polynomial is separable and the eventual expansion has the form
 615:   \(U_n=\sum_\gamma A_\gamma\gamma^n\), with \(\gamma\) running over the
 616:   conjugates of \(\beta\). Since \(U_n\in\mathbb Q\), Galois automorphisms
 617:   send \(A_\beta\) to the corresponding \(A_{\sigma(\beta)}\). If
 618:   \(A_\beta=0\), every conjugate coefficient would vanish, contradicting
 619:   that this polynomial is the minimal tail annihilator of the nonzero
 620:   sequence. Thus \(A_\beta\ne0\), and every other conjugate has modulus
 621:   strictly less than \(\beta\), proving the limit. Taking
 622:   \(t\)-th roots in the preceding inequalities therefore yields
 623:   \(b=\beta^D\).
 624: 
 625:   If \(\beta\) had degree greater than one, any other conjugate
 626:   \(\beta'\) would satisfy \((\beta')^D=b\), and hence
 627:   \(\abs{\beta'}=b^{1/D}=\beta\), contrary to the defining Perron
 628:   inequality.  Thus \(\beta\) has degree one; being an algebraic integer
 629:   greater than one, it belongs to \(\mathbb Z_{\ge2}\).  This proves
 630:   \textup{(ii)}\(\Rightarrow\)\textup{(iii)}.
 631: 
 632:   Conversely, suppose \(\beta=B\in\mathbb Z_{\ge2}\).  Its minimal
 633:   polynomial is \(X-B\), so, after the minimal recurrence threshold
 634:   \(J_{\mathrm r}\), one has \(U_{n+1}=BU_n\).  The greedy representation
 635:   of \(U_n\), in the LSD-first convention, is \(0^n1\).  Taking
 636:   \[
 637:     a=0^{J_{\mathrm r}},\quad k=1,\quad
 638:     u_1=w_1=s_1=\varepsilon,\quad v_1=0,\quad u_2=1
 639:   \]
 640:   gives canonical words \(W(t)=0^{J_{\mathrm r}+t}1\) and
 641:   \[
 642:     \val_U(W(t))=U_{J_{\mathrm r}}B^t.
 643:   \]
 644:   This is a geometric synchronized scheme, proving
 645:   \textup{(iii)}\(\Rightarrow\)\textup{(ii)}.  The universal assertion
 646:   for nonintegral \(\beta\) is the negation of \textup{(i)}.  Finally, a
 647:   Perron minimal polynomial has degree one exactly when its root is an
 648:   integer, which gives the stated terminating decision procedure.
 649: \end{proof}
 650: 
 651: \begin{remark}[Exact open interfaces]
 652:   \label{rem:pisot-open-interfaces}
 653:   The terminology for linear numeration systems with Perron or Pisot
 654:   recurrence is not uniform.
 655:   \Cref{cor:linear-perron-prime-cf-immunity} covers the standard
 656:   positional linear \(U\)-systems just specified, including integer
 657:   bases, Zeckendorf/Fibonacci, Pell, higher-order Pisot recurrences, and
 658:   non-Pisot Perron examples such as \(X^2-5X+5\).
 659:   It does not assert the theorem for arbitrary real-base
 660:   \(\beta\)-shift representations, non-positional abstract numeration
 661:   systems, redundant noninjective digit systems, or systems lacking an
 662:   eventual integral recurrence and strict length ordering. Extending
 663:   the argument to any of those classes requires a stationary finite
 664:   block action that is invertible modulo represented primes.
 665: 
 666:   For a fixed nonunit recurrence witness, the ambient action can be
 667:   singular modulo primes dividing \(a_0\), so the unrestricted unit-system
 668:   divisibility tree need not follow. The witness-relative surviving
 669:   statement is \Cref{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}: every infinite
 670:   MCFL must acquire unbounded prime support outside that finite set or
 671:   unbounded valuation at one of its primes. The invariant restricted
 672:   action and the failure of arbitrary recurrence witnesses to classify
 673:   escape behavior are distinguished in
 674:   \Cref{prop:recurrence-witness-separation}.
 675:   Within greedy linear numeration bases with Perron recurrence, the residual
 676:   alternative is classified
 677:   by \Cref{cor:linear-perron-bounded-support-classification}: it occurs exactly
 678:   for an integral dominant root.  The ordinary-base example preceding
 679:   \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain} shows that the valuation
 680:   alternative cannot be removed.
 681: \end{remark}

   1: \begin{definition}[Geometric synchronized scheme]
   2:   \label{def:geometric-synchronized-scheme}
   3:   A geometric synchronized scheme for \(U\) consists of
   4:   \(a\in A^{J_{\mathrm r}}\), an integer \(k\ge1\), integers
   5:   \(c\ge1,b\ge2\), and words
   6:   \[
   7:     u_1,\ldots,u_{k+1},\quad
   8:     v_1,\ldots,v_k,\quad w_1,\ldots,w_k,\quad
   9:     s_1,\ldots,s_k\in A^\ast,
  10:   \]
  11:   such that
  12:   \[
  13:     W(t)=a u_1v_1^tw_1s_1^tu_2\cdots
  14:       u_kv_k^tw_ks_k^tu_{k+1}\in\mathcal R_U
  15:       \qquad(t\ge0),
  16:   \]
  17:   \[
  18:     \sum_{j=1}^k(\abs{v_j}+\abs{s_j})>0,
  19:     \qquad \val_U(W(t))=cb^t\quad(t\ge0).
  20:   \]
  21: \end{definition}
  22: 
  23: \begin{lemma}[Arithmetic input of Evertse]
  24:   \label{lem:evertse-quotient-support-input}
  25:   Let \(K\) be a number field. For \(x\in K^\times\), let \(P_K(x)\)
  26:   be the largest norm of a finite prime ideal \(\mathfrak p\) for which
  27:   \(\operatorname{ord}_{\mathfrak p}(x)\ne0\), with \(P_K(x)=1\) when
  28:   \(x\) is a unit. If \(Z=(Z(n))_{n\ge0}\) is a nondegenerate linear
  29:   recurrence over \(K\) whose minimal shift-annihilating polynomial has
  30:   at least two distinct nonzero roots, then
  31:   \[
  32:     \lim_{\substack{r\to\infty\\ r>s\ge0,\ Z(s)\ne0}}
  33:       P_K\!\left(\frac{Z(r)}{Z(s)}\right)=\infty.
  34:   \]
  35:   In particular, a rational-integer recurrence with no zero terms, all
  36:   supported on one finite set of rational primes, cannot be both
  37:   nondegenerate and have at least two characteristic roots.
  38: \end{lemma}
  39: 
  40: \begin{proof}
  41:   The displayed uniform limit is exactly
  42:   \cite[Theorem~3]{Evertse1984}. For the consequence, let \(S\) be the
  43:   finite rational-prime support and choose a number field \(K\) containing
  44:   the characteristic roots and coefficients. If \(Z(r),Z(s)\ne0\), the
  45:   principal fractional ideal generated by \(Z(r)/Z(s)\) is supported
  46:   only at prime ideals above primes in \(S\). Every prime ideal
  47:   \(\mathfrak p\) above \(p\in S\) has
  48:   \[
  49:     \operatorname N\mathfrak p=p^{f(\mathfrak p/p)}
  50:       \le p^{[K:\mathbb Q]}.
  51:   \]
  52:   Consequently
  53:   \[
  54:     P_K\!\left(\frac{Z(r)}{Z(s)}\right)
  55:       \le \max\bigl(\{p^{[K:\mathbb Q]}:p\in S\}\cup\{1\}\bigr)
  56:   \]
  57:   for every \(r,s\) with nonzero denominator. This fixed bound
  58:   contradicts Evertse's limit when there are at least two characteristic
  59:   roots. Here and below, ``characteristic roots of a sequence'' always
  60:   means the roots of its minimal shift-annihilating polynomial, not roots
  61:   introduced by a nonminimal recurrence witness.
  62: \end{proof}
  63: 
  64: \begin{lemma}[Increasing \(S\)-unit recurrences]
  65:   \label{lem:s-unit-increasing-recurrence-rigidity}
  66:   Let \(Y=(Y(n))_{n\ge0}\) be a strictly increasing sequence of positive
  67:   rational integers satisfying a linear recurrence. If all prime
  68:   divisors of all \(Y(n)\) belong to one finite set, then there are
  69:   integers \(r\ge0,h\ge1,c\ge1\), and \(b\ge2\) such that
  70:   \[
  71:     Y(r+hn)=cb^n\qquad(n\ge0).
  72:   \]
  73: \end{lemma}
  74: 
  75: \begin{proof}
  76:   Write the standard exponential-polynomial expansion, after discarding
  77:   the contribution of the zero characteristic root and a finite initial
  78:   segment, as
  79:   \[
  80:     Y(n)=\sum_{i=1}^m f_i(n)\alpha_i^n,
  81:   \]
  82:   where the \(\alpha_i\) are distinct nonzero algebraic numbers and the
  83:   nonzero \(f_i\) are algebraic polynomials. Partition the roots by
  84:   \(\alpha_i\sim\alpha_j\) when \(\alpha_i/\alpha_j\) is a root of
  85:   unity. Choose \(h_0\ge1\) divisible by the orders of all such
  86:   quotients. For each \(j\in\{0,\ldots,h_0-1\}\), substitution of
  87:   \(n=j+h_0t\) and collection within the equivalence classes gives
  88:   \[
  89:     Y(j+h_0t)=\sum_C g_{C,j}(t)\beta_C^t,
  90:     \qquad \beta_C=\alpha_i^{h_0}\quad(i\in C).
  91:   \]
  92:   Delete the identically zero summands. Distinct remaining \(\beta_C\)
  93:   have quotient that is not a root of unity. Thus every residue
  94:   subsequence is a nonzero nondegenerate recurrence; it is nonzero
  95:   because all of its terms are positive.
  96: 
  97:   Every term of every residue subsequence is a nonzero rational integer
  98:   supported on the prescribed finite set. If a residue subsequence had
  99:   at least two characteristic roots, the consequence in
 100:   \Cref{lem:evertse-quotient-support-input} would contradict the fixed
 101:   support of all its term quotients. Hence every residue subsequence has
 102:   exactly one characteristic root and therefore has the form
 103:   \[
 104:     Z(n)=f(n)\lambda^n
 105:   \]
 106:   with one characteristic root.
 107: 
 108:   Choose one residue subsequence and shift its index if necessary so
 109:   that the displayed expansion holds from \(n=0\), and put
 110:   \(e=\deg f\). Let \(E\) denote the forward shift. Over
 111:   \(\overline{\mathbb Q}\), finite differences give
 112:   \[
 113:     (E-\lambda)^{e+1}Z=0,
 114:     \qquad (E-\lambda)^eZ\ne0,
 115:   \]
 116:   because the \(e\)-th difference of the nonzero degree-\(e\) polynomial
 117:   \(f\) is a nonzero constant. Hence the monic minimal shift-annihilator
 118:   over \(\overline{\mathbb Q}\) is exactly
 119:   \((X-\lambda)^{e+1}\), and the Hankel rank of \(Z\) over that field is
 120:   \(e+1\).
 121: 
 122:   The Hankel matrix has rational entries, and a rational matrix has the
 123:   same rank over \(\mathbb Q\) and over \(\overline{\mathbb Q}\).
 124:   Therefore a minimal dependence among its shift columns gives a monic
 125:   polynomial \(P\in\mathbb Q[X]\) of the same degree \(e+1\). After
 126:   extending scalars, minimality and equality of degrees force
 127:   \[
 128:     P(X)=(X-\lambda)^{e+1}.
 129:   \]
 130:   For every
 131:   \(\sigma\in\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/\mathbb Q)\), the
 132:   polynomial \(P\) is fixed and has only the root \(\lambda\); thus
 133:   \(\sigma(\lambda)=\lambda\). Hence \(\lambda\in\mathbb Q\). Now
 134:   \(f(n)=Z(n)/\lambda^n\in\mathbb Q\)
 135:   for every \(n\), and interpolation from \(\deg(f)+1\) integer
 136:   arguments gives \(f\in\mathbb Q[X]\). If \(f\) were nonconstant, clear its
 137:   denominators to obtain a nonconstant \(g\in\mathbb Z[X]\). The
 138:   identity \(Z(n)=f(n)\lambda^n\) would confine every prime divisor of
 139:   every nonzero \(g(n)\) to a fixed finite set. Indeed, if
 140:   \(\lambda=a/b\) is in lowest terms and \(g=Df\), then

 succeeded in 195ms:
 141:   \[
 142:     g(n)a^n=Db^nZ(n).
 143:   \]
 144:   Thus that fixed set is the union of the prescribed prime set and the
 145:   prime divisors of \(Dab\). This contradicts Schur's theorem that a
 146:   nonconstant integer polynomial has values divisible by infinitely
 147:   many primes; see, for example, \cite{HardyWright2008}. Hence \(f\) is
 148:   constant.
 149: 
 150:   We have therefore \(Z(n)=c\lambda^n\), where
 151:   \(c=Z(0)\) is a positive integer. If \(\lambda=a/b\) in lowest terms,
 152:   integrality for every \(n\) forces \(b^n\mid c\) for every \(n\), so
 153:   \(b=1\). Positivity and strict increase then force
 154:   \(\lambda\in\mathbb Z_{\ge2}\). Translating the chosen residue and
 155:   index shift back to \(Y\) supplies the asserted \(r\) and \(h\).
 156: \end{proof}
 157: 
 158: \begin{theorem}[Geometric-ray characterization]
 159:   \label{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}
 160:   For a canonical eventually recurrent numeration system satisfying
 161:   \textup{(U1)--(U4)}, the following are equivalent.
 162:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 163:     \item There are a finite \(k\) and an infinite \(k\)-MCFL
 164:     \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) such that
 165:     \[
 166:       \sup_{w\in L}\omega_{S_{\mathrm r}^c}(\val_U(w))<\infty.
 167:     \]
 168:     \item The system admits a geometric synchronized scheme in the
 169:     sense of \Cref{def:geometric-synchronized-scheme}.
 170:     \item The language \(\mathcal R_U\) contains an infinite linear MCFL
 171:     \(L_0=\{W(t):t\ge0\}\) of finite fan-out for which there are
 172:     \(c\ge1,b\ge2\) satisfying
 173:     \[
 174:       \val_U(W(t))=cb^t\qquad(t\ge0).
 175:     \]
 176:   \end{enumerate}
 177: \end{theorem}
 178: 
 179: \begin{proof}
 180:   Suppose first that \textup{(i)} holds. Use the minimal tail recurrence
 181:   as the witness in
 182:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}, and write its resulting
 183:   ray and strictly increasing value sequence as \(W(t)\) and \(N(t)\).
 184:   Let
 185:   \[
 186:     K_0=\max_{t\ge0}\omega_{S_{\mathrm r}^c}(N(t)),
 187:   \]
 188:   and choose \(t_0\) attaining the maximum. Let \(T\) be the set of
 189:   primes outside \(S_{\mathrm r}\) that divide \(N(t_0)\), and put
 190:   \(q=\prod_{\ell\in T}\ell\). If \(T\ne\varnothing\), take the return
 191:   time \(H=H(q)\) from
 192:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}; if \(T=\varnothing\),
 193:   put \(H=1\). Every prime in \(T\) divides every \(N(t_0+nH)\). A prime
 194:   divisor outside \(S_{\mathrm r}\cup T\) would therefore give at least
 195:   \(K_0+1\) distinct prime divisors outside \(S_{\mathrm r}\), contrary
 196:   to the definition of \(K_0\). Thus all terms of
 197:   \[
 198:     Y(n)=N(t_0+nH)
 199:   \]
 200:   are supported on the fixed finite set \(S_{\mathrm r}\cup T\).
 201: 
 202:   To make the termwise-product closure explicit, put
 203:   \(n_0=d_{\mathrm r}+1\), list the \(2k\) powered affine blocks as
 204:   \(B_1,\ldots,B_{2k}\), and form
 205:   \[
 206:     K=B_1\otimes\cdots\otimes B_{2k}.
 207:   \]
 208:   Since \(K^t=B_1^t\otimes\cdots\otimes B_{2k}^t\), expansion of the
 209:   interspersed constant block matrices expresses \(N(t)\) as a fixed
 210:   linear functional of \(K^t\). Cayley--Hamilton therefore gives a
 211:   recurrence of order at most
 212:   \[
 213:     R=n_0^{2k}=(d_{\mathrm r}+1)^{2k}.
 214:   \]
 215:   Arithmetic subsequences of a recurrence are recurrences, so \(Y\) is a
 216:   strictly increasing positive integer recurrence. By
 217:   \Cref{lem:s-unit-increasing-recurrence-rigidity}, there are
 218:   \(r\ge0,h\ge1,c\ge1,b\ge2\) such that
 219:   \(N(t_\ast+h_\ast n)=cb^n\), where
 220:   \(t_\ast=t_0+Hr\) and \(h_\ast=Hh\).
 221: 
 222:   In the synchronized factorization, replace
 223:   \[
 224:     u_j\ \text{by}\ u_jv_j^{t_\ast},\qquad
 225:     v_j\ \text{by}\ v_j^{h_\ast},\qquad
 226:     w_j\ \text{by}\ w_js_j^{t_\ast},\qquad
 227:     s_j\ \text{by}\ s_j^{h_\ast}.
 228:   \]
 229:   This absorbs the fixed powers and parametrizes exactly the subray
 230:   \(W(t_\ast+h_\ast n)\). Its total pumped length remains positive, so
 231:   it is a geometric synchronized scheme. This proves
 232:   \textup{(i)}\(\Rightarrow\)\textup{(ii)}.
 233: 
 234:   The implication \textup{(ii)}\(\Rightarrow\)\textup{(iii)} is
 235:   \Cref{lem:synchronized-ray-linear-mcfg}. Finally, under
 236:   \textup{(iii)}, the language \(L_0\) is an infinite MCFL and
 237:   \[
 238:     \omega_{S_{\mathrm r}^c}(cb^t)\le\omega(cb)
 239:     \qquad(t\ge0),
 240:   \]
 241:   proving \textup{(i)}.
 242: \end{proof}
 243: 
 244: \begin{lemma}[Prime support of the geometric ratio]
 245:   \label{lem:geometric-ratio-tail-support}
 246:   In every geometric synchronized scheme
 247:   \(\val_U(W(t))=cb^t\), each prime divisor of \(b\) divides the
 248:   constant coefficient \(c_0\) of the minimal tail recurrence.  Equivalently,
 249:   \[
 250:     \operatorname{rad}(b)\mid\operatorname{rad}(\abs{c_0}).
 251:   \]
 252: \end{lemma}
 253: 
 254: \begin{proof}
 255:   Suppose that a prime \(p\mid b\) does not divide \(c_0\), put
 256:   \(e=v_p(c)\), and take the allowed modulus \(q=p^{e+1}\).  Every
 257:   nonempty block matrix among the \(M_{v_j}\) and \(M_{s_j}\) is invertible
 258:   modulo \(q\) by \Cref{lem:affine-recurrence-block-action}.  A common
 259:   multiple \(H\ge1\) of their orders gives, by the matrix product defining
 260:   the scheme exactly as in the proof of
 261:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit},
 262:   \[
 263:     \val_U(W(t+H))\equiv\val_U(W(t))\pmod q
 264:     \qquad(t\ge0).
 265:   \]
 266:   At \(t=0\) this says
 267:   \[
 268:     cb^H\equiv c\pmod {p^{e+1}}.
 269:   \]
 270:   But \(p\mid b\) implies \(p\nmid b^H-1\), so
 271:   \(v_p(c(b^H-1))=e\), contradicting the displayed congruence.
 272: \end{proof}
 273: 
 274: \begin{corollary}[Positive semidecidability on promised presentations]
 275:   \label{cor:bounded-outside-support-semidecidable}
 276:   On the promised class of valid presentations satisfying
 277:   \textup{(U1)--(U4)}, suppose the finite digit alphabet, recurrence and
 278:   initial data, and a DFA recognizing \(\mathcal R_U\) are supplied,
 279:   together with a supplied or effectively certified minimal tail
 280:   recurrence and a valid threshold \(J_{\mathrm r}\). Then the set of

 succeeded in 195ms:
 281:   positive promised instances of the property in
 282:   \Cref{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}\textup{(i)} is
 283:   recursively enumerable, equivalently it has a \(\Sigma^0_1\) upper
 284:   bound. No recognition procedure for membership in the promised input
 285:   class is asserted.
 286: \end{corollary}
 287: 
 288: \begin{proof}
 289:   Enumerate \(k\ge1\), \(a\in A^{J_{\mathrm r}}\), and all finite tuples
 290:   of words occurring in
 291:   \Cref{def:geometric-synchronized-scheme}, rejecting tuples of zero
 292:   total pumped length. For a fixed tuple, whether the DFA accepts
 293:   \(W(t)\) for every \(t\ge0\) is decidable: the tuple of powers of the
 294:   finitely many pumped-block transformations is eventually periodic in
 295:   the finite transition monoid, and its preperiod and period are found
 296:   by exhaustive iteration.
 297: 
 298:   The affine matrices construct an exact linear recurrence for
 299:   \[
 300:     N(t)=\val_U(W(t)).
 301:   \]
 302:   With \(d_{\mathrm r}\) the minimal tail order, the Kronecker construction
 303:   in the proof of
 304:   \Cref{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray} has dimension
 305:   \[
 306:     R=(d_{\mathrm r}+1)^{2k};
 307:   \]
 308:   it is computable from the candidate and is an upper bound for the
 309:   recurrence order of \(N(t)\). For a tuple accepted at every parameter,
 310:   compute \(c=N(0)\). The only
 311:   possible integer ratio is \(b=N(1)/N(0)\). Check that
 312:   \(b\in\mathbb Z_{\ge2}\), and then decide whether the recurrence
 313:   \(N(t+1)-bN(t)\) is identically zero by checking its first \(R\) terms;
 314:   Cayley--Hamilton makes those \(R\) zero values sufficient. Thus each
 315:   candidate scheme is decidable, and the enumeration halts exactly on
 316:   positive promised instances by
 317:   \Cref{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}. No claim of a
 318:   decision procedure for negative instances is made.
 319: \end{proof}
 320: 
 321: \begin{remark}[Exact algorithmic boundary]
 322:   \label{rem:general-decision-interface}
 323:   The preceding corollary does not yield a decision procedure. To turn
 324:   its enumeration into one, it would suffice to compute from the input
 325:   DFA and minimal tail recurrence a bound \(B\) such that every positive
 326:   instance has a geometric synchronized scheme with fan-out and total
 327:   constant and pumped-block length at most \(B\). No such compression
 328:   follows from DFA pumping: replacing a long loop by a shorter word with
 329:   the same DFA endpoints need not preserve its affine recurrence matrix,
 330:   and can therefore destroy the identity
 331:   \(\val_U(W(t+1))=b\val_U(W(t))\).
 332: 
 333:   The neighboring decision theory does not close this step. Skolem asks
 334:   whether one term of a recurrence vanishes, while Positivity and Ultimate
 335:   Positivity ask for signs of all or all sufficiently late terms; these
 336:   problems remain open in unrestricted order
 337:   \cite{OuaknineWorrell2012,AkshayBazilleGenestVahanwala2024}.
 338:   Tarasov--Vyalyi relate an orbit-hitting problem to regular-language
 339:   intersection \cite{TarasovVyalyi2011}, while unrestricted multiplicative
 340:   matrix equations include undecidable families
 341:   \cite{BellEtAl2008MatrixEquations}. Our candidate check instead asks
 342:   whether the explicitly constructed recurrence
 343:   \(N(t+1)-bN(t)\) is identically zero; no reduction in either direction is
 344:   claimed. None of these observations is a reduction for the companion-form
 345:   digit matrices here, and conditions
 346:   \textup{(U1)--(U3)} must also be preserved. Thus general decidability,
 347:   undecidability, and any hardness classification remain open; the proved
 348:   effective statement is exactly the positive semidecision above. The
 349:   Perron-recurrence subclass of greedy linear numeration bases is resolved
 350:   separately below.
 351: \end{remark}
 352: 
 353: \begin{theorem}[Arbitrarily deep quotient congruences]
 354:   \label{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain}
 355:   Suppose \(\abs{a_0}=1\), and let \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) be an
 356:   infinite MCFL. For arbitrary sequences \(M_i\ge1\) and \(r_i\ge1\),
 357:   \(L\) contains a strictly increasing sequence \(N_i\) such that
 358:   \[
 359:     N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i>1,\qquad
 360:     Q_i\equiv1\pmod {M_iN_i^{r_i}}.
 361:   \]
 362:   If \(M_i\) is divisible by every prime at most \(B_i\), then every
 363:   prime divisor of \(Q_i\) exceeds \(B_i\).
 364: \end{theorem}
 365: 
 366: \begin{proof}
 367:   Take the fixed orbit \(N(t)\) from
 368:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}, discarding finitely many
 369:   initial parameters so that \(N(t)\ge2\). Having selected
 370:   \(N_i=N(t_i)\), apply the synchronized congruence with
 371:   \[
 372:     q_i=M_iN_i^{r_i+1}.
 373:   \]
 374:   Set \(t_{i+1}=t_i+H(q_i)\). Strict increase and the congruence give
 375:   \[
 376:     N_{i+1}=N_i+c_iM_iN_i^{r_i+1}
 377:            =N_i(1+c_iM_iN_i^{r_i})
 378:   \]
 379:   for an integer \(c_i\ge1\). This proves the first assertion. Under
 380:   the last hypothesis, \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod p\) for every prime
 381:   \(p\le B_i\), so none of those primes divides \(Q_i\).
 382: \end{proof}
 383: 
 384: \begin{theorem}[Induced full divisibility tree]
 385:   \label{thm:unit-mcfl-divisibility-tree}
 386:   Under the hypotheses of
 387:   \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain}, there is an injection
 388:   \[
 389:     \Phi:\NN^{<\omega}\longrightarrow L
 390:   \]
 391:   such that
 392:   \[
 393:     \val_U(\Phi(\sigma))\mid\val_U(\Phi(\tau))
 394:       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \sigma\preceq\tau,
 395:   \]
 396:   where \(\preceq\) denotes the prefix order. The quotients attached to
 397:   distinct edges can be chosen pairwise coprime. Moreover, given an
 398:   arbitrary prescribed threshold for each edge, every prime divisor of
 399:   its quotient can be required to exceed that threshold.
 400: \end{theorem}
 401: 
 402: \begin{proof}
 403:   Use the fixed strictly increasing orbit from
 404:   \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}. Here is an explicit
 405:   parent-before-child enumeration. If \(p_i\) is the \(i\)-th prime, encode
 406:   \(\sigma=(\sigma_0,\ldots,\sigma_{m-1})\) by
 407:   \[
 408:     \kappa(\sigma)=2^m\prod_{i=0}^{m-1}p_{i+2}^{\sigma_i+1}.
 409:   \]
 410:   Unique factorization makes \(\kappa\) injective, and
 411:   \(\kappa(\sigma^\frown j)>\kappa(\sigma)\). Enumerating the nonroot
 412:   nodes, equivalently their incoming edges, by increasing \(\kappa\)
 413:   therefore has order type \(\omega\) and places every parent before each
 414:   child. Map
 415:   the root to any orbit word of value \(R_0\ge2\). Suppose finitely many
 416:   edges have been assigned, and consider a new edge from a parent of
 417:   value \(P\). Let \(R\) be divisible by \(R_0\), by every previously
 418:   assigned edge quotient, and by every prime not exceeding the prescribed
 419:   threshold for the new edge. Apply the synchronized orbit congruence
 420:   modulo \(P^2R\) at the parameter of the parent. A sufficiently large
 421:   positive multiple of the return time gives an unused orbit word of
 422:   value \(P'>P\) with
 423:   \[
 424:     P'\equiv P\pmod {P^2R}.
 425:   \]
 426:   Hence \(P'=PQ\), where \(Q>1\) and \(Q\equiv1\pmod {PR}\). In
 427:   particular, \(Q\) is coprime to the root and to every earlier edge
 428:   quotient, and no prime up to the prescribed threshold divides \(Q\).
 429: 
 430:   Recursion defines \(\Phi\) on every node. The value at a node is the
 431:   root value times the product of the edge quotients along its path.
 432:   Since the root and all distinct edge quotients are pairwise coprime,
 433:   the value at \(\sigma\) divides the value at \(\tau\) exactly when the
 434:   root-to-\(\sigma\) edge set is contained in the root-to-\(\tau\) edge
 435:   set. For two nodes of a rooted tree, containment of their root-path edge
 436:   sets holds exactly when the first node is an ancestor of the second;
 437:   here this is precisely \(\sigma\preceq\tau\). This also proves injectivity.
 438: \end{proof}
 439: 
 440: \begin{theorem}[Prime immunity for recurrent numeration systems]

# Monatshefte fuer Mathematik submission metadata

Journal: Monatshefte fuer Mathematik

Article type: Original research article

Title: Prime support and multiple-context-free languages in recurrent numeration

Authors:

1. Haobo Ma, AELF PTE LTD., Singapore, auric@aelf.io
2. Wenlin Zhang, National University of Singapore, Singapore,
   e1327962@u.nus.edu (corresponding author)

## Referee-facing upload set

The submission is incomplete unless every item below is delivered to the
editorial office and visible in the referee view.

1. `main.pdf`: primary manuscript.
2. `supplement.pdf`: Supplementary Information, designated Online Resource 1.
3. `reproducibility.zip`: scripts, unit tests, archived outputs, literature
   audit, checksums, and `REPRODUCE.md`, designated Online Resource 2.
4. `source.zip`: all LaTeX sources required to compile both PDFs, plus
   `README.md`, `REPRODUCE.md`, and `references.tex`.
5. `cover_letter.txt`: journal-specific cover letter.
6. `submission_metadata.md`: this upload manifest.

The unpacked `artifacts/` directory is also present in `submission_bundle/`
so that each script and each readable text output can be uploaded separately
if the portal does not expose files inside an archive to referees.

## Supplement policy

Checked 2026-08-15 against the official journal instructions:
https://link.springer.com/journal/605/submission-guidelines

The Monatshefte instructions contain a dedicated "Supplementary Information
(SI)" section. They accept supplementary PDFs, specialized formats including
`.tex`, and collections in `.zip` or `.gz` format. They require a specific
mention in the article using the designation "Online Resource". The article
therefore names the supplement Online Resource 1, and the reproducibility
archive is Online Resource 2. No content needs to be migrated back into the
main manuscript.

## Dependency statement

The primary manuscript is mathematically self-contained. It repeats the
Zeckendorf affine block lemma needed by its theorem chain and has no numbered
`\suppref` pointer. Online Resource 1 contains independent finite-state,
Zeckendorf, and analytic results. It is part of the upload because the article
mentions it and because the reproducibility package verifies computations
associated with the submitted project.

## Verified expected results

- `main.pdf`: clean XeLaTeX build, no undefined references or citations, no
  multiply defined labels.
- `supplement.pdf`: same clean-build standard.
- Verifier: 6 systems, 2282 affine cases, zero failure counters, final line
  `OVERALL: PASS`.
- Unit tests: 19 tests, final result `OK`.
- Integrity: every digest in `artifacts/SHA256SUMS` matches.

Before completing the portal submission, open every attachment through the
referee-facing file list and confirm that Online Resources 1 and 2 are visible.

 551: 
 552: The second alternative is necessary. In ordinary base \(b\), where
 553: \(a_0=b\), the regular LSD-first language
 554: \(\{0^n1:n\ge0\}\) represents \(b^n\). Its distinct prime support is
 555: fixed, while \(v_p(b^n)\) is unbounded for every \(p\mid b\).
 556: 
 557: \begin{proposition}[Recurrence-witness inflation and strict separation]
 558:   \label{prop:recurrence-witness-separation}
 559:   If a tail has minimal recurrence polynomial \(\mu_U\), then for every
 560:   prime \(p\) it also has a recurrence witness with characteristic
 561:   polynomial \((X-p)\mu_U(X)\).  When \(\abs{\mu_U(0)}=1\), the trailing
 562:   coefficient of this witness has absolute value \(p\).
 563: 
 564:   Moreover, there are two canonical eventually recurrent
 565:   numeration systems with regular representation languages and recurrence
 566:   witnesses whose trailing-coefficient prime set is \(\{p\}\), but with
 567:   opposite bounded-outside-prime escape behavior:
 568:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 569:     \item ordinary base \(p\) contains the infinite regular language
 570:     \(\{0^n1:n\ge0\}\), whose values are \(p^n\);
 571:     \item the Zeckendorf system, with \(F_0=0,F_1=1\) and
 572:     \(F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_n\), equipped with the order-three recurrence
 573:     witness
 574:     \[
 575:       F_{n+3}=-pF_n+(1-p)F_{n+1}+(p+1)F_{n+2},
 576:     \]
 577:     contains no infinite MCFL \(L\) for which
 578:     \[
 579:       \sup_{w\in L}\omega_{\{p\}^c}(\val(w))<\infty.
 580:     \]
 581:   \end{enumerate}
 582:   Thus the prime divisors of an arbitrarily selected trailing coefficient
 583:   do not determine the escape behavior of the numeration system.
 584: \end{proposition}
 585: 
 586: \begin{proof}
 587:   The polynomial identity \(\mu_U(C)z_J=0\) remains valid after
 588:   multiplication by \(X-p\).  In the companion convention, the trailing
 589:   recurrence coefficient is the negative of the constant term of the
 590:   characteristic polynomial, proving the first assertion.
 591: 
 592:   The base-\(p\) assertion is the preceding example.  For the Zeckendorf
 593:   sequence, multiplication of its minimal polynomial by \(X-p\) gives
 594:   \[
 595:     (X-p)(X^2-X-1)
 596:       =X^3-(p+1)X^2+(p-1)X+p,
 597:   \]
 598:   which is exactly the displayed recurrence and has trailing coefficient
 599:   \(-p\).  This changes neither the sequence nor its regular canonical
 600:   representation language.  On the other hand, its minimal recurrence
 601:   has unit trailing coefficient.  If the displayed supremum were bounded
 602:   by \(K\), then
 603:   \(\omega(\val(w))\le K+1\) on \(L\), contrary to the unit-recurrence
 604:   conclusion of
 605:   \Cref{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}.  This proves the separation.
 606: \end{proof}
 607: 
 608: Let
 609: \[
 610:   \mu_U(X)=X^{d_{\mathrm r}}-c_{d_{\mathrm r}-1}X^{d_{\mathrm r}-1}
 611:     -\cdots-c_1X-c_0
 612: \]
 613: be the minimal recurrence polynomial of a tail of \(U\). Since it
 614: divides the characteristic polynomial of a recurrence allowed in
 615: \textup{(U4)}, its constant term is nonzero. Enlarge the recurrence
 616: threshold to \(J_{\mathrm r}\) so that this minimal recurrence holds for
 617: every \(n\ge J_{\mathrm r}\), and put
 618: \[
 619:   S_{\mathrm r}=\{p:p\text{ is prime and }p\mid c_0\}.
 620: \]

codex
The technical path closes on paper: after taking a Perron power, each residue class has a dominant coefficient; positivity makes those coefficients nonnegative, and strict increase of the place values propagates one positive coefficient cyclically to all residues. This yields the required global root limit without assuming strict dominance. I’ll cite the standard weak-Perron spectral characterization and keep the paper’s quantifier ceiling explicit; the result remains a classification of one synchronized ray, not global control of a language or two representations.

---
name: test-driven-development
description: Use when implementing any feature or bugfix, before writing implementation code
---

# Test-Driven Development (TDD)

## Overview

Write the test first. Watch it fail. Write minimal code to pass.

**Core principle:** If you didn't watch the test fail, you don't know if it tests the right thing.

**Violating the letter of the rules is violating the spirit of the rules.**

## When to Use

**Always:**
- New features
- Bug fixes
- Refactoring
- Behavior changes

**Exceptions (ask your human partner):**
- Throwaway prototypes
- Generated code
- Configuration files

Thinking "skip TDD just this once"? Stop. That's rationalization.

## The Iron Law

```
NO PRODUCTION CODE WITHOUT A FAILING TEST FIRST
```

Write code before the test? Delete it. Start over.

**No exceptions:**
- Don't keep it as "reference"
- Don't "adapt" it while writing tests
- Don't look at it
- Delete means delete

Implement fresh from tests. Period.

## Red-Green-Refactor

```dot
digraph tdd_cycle {
    rankdir=LR;
    red [label="RED\nWrite failing test", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ffcccc"];
    verify_red [label="Verify fails\ncorrectly", shape=diamond];
    green [label="GREEN\nMinimal code", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ccffcc"];
    verify_green [label="Verify passes\nAll green", shape=diamond];
    refactor [label="REFACTOR\nClean up", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ccccff"];
    next [label="Next", shape=ellipse];

    red -> verify_red;
    verify_red -> green [label="yes"];
    verify_red -> red [label="wrong\nfailure"];
    green -> verify_green;
    verify_green -> refactor [label="yes"];
    verify_green -> green [label="no"];
    refactor -> verify_green [label="stay\ngreen"];
    verify_green -> next;
    next -> red;
}
```

### RED - Write Failing Test

Write one minimal test showing what should happen.

<Good>
```typescript
test('retries failed operations 3 times', async () => {
  let attempts = 0;
  const operation = () => {
    attempts++;
    if (attempts < 3) throw new Error('fail');
    return 'success';
  };

  const result = await retryOperation(operation);

  expect(result).toBe('success');
  expect(attempts).toBe(3);
});
```
Clear name, tests real behavior, one thing
</Good>

<Bad>
```typescript
test('retry works', async () => {
  const mock = jest.fn()
    .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error())
    .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error())
    .mockResolvedValueOnce('success');
  await retryOperation(mock);
  expect(mock).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(3);
});
```
Vague name, tests mock not code
</Bad>

**Requirements:**
- One behavior
- Clear name
- Real code (no mocks unless unavoidable)

### Verify RED - Watch It Fail

**MANDATORY. Never skip.**

```bash
npm test path/to/test.test.ts
```

Confirm:
- Test fails (not errors)
- Failure message is expected
- Fails because feature missing (not typos)

**Test passes?** You're testing existing behavior. Fix test.

**Test errors?** Fix error, re-run until it fails correctly.

### GREEN - Minimal Code

Write simplest code to pass the test.

<Good>
```typescript
async function retryOperation<T>(fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
  for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
    try {
      return await fn();
    } catch (e) {
      if (i === 2) throw e;
    }
  }
  throw new Error('unreachable');
}
```
Just enough to pass
</Good>

<Bad>
```typescript
async function retryOperation<T>(
  fn: () => Promise<T>,
  options?: {
    maxRetries?: number;
    backoff?: 'linear' | 'exponential';
    onRetry?: (attempt: number) => void;
  }
): Promise<T> {
  // YAGNI
}
```
Over-engineered
</Bad>

Don't add features, refactor other code, or "improve" beyond the test.

### Verify GREEN - Watch It Pass

**MANDATORY.**

```bash
npm test path/to/test.test.ts
```

Confirm:
- Test passes
- Other tests still pass
- Output pristine (no errors, warnings)

**Test fails?** Fix code, not test.

**Other tests fail?** Fix now.

### REFACTOR - Clean Up

After green only:
- Remove duplication
- Improve names
- Extract helpers

Keep tests green. Don't add behavior.

### Repeat

Next failing test for next feature.

## Good Tests

| Quality | Good | Bad |
|---------|------|-----|
| **Minimal** | One thing. "and" in name? Split it. | `test('validates email and domain and whitespace')` |
| **Clear** | Name describes behavior | `test('test1')` |
| **Shows intent** | Demonstrates desired API | Obscures what code should do |

## Why Order Matters

**"I'll write tests after to verify it works"**

Tests written after code pass immediately. Passing immediately proves nothing:
- Might test wrong thing
- Might test implementation, not behavior
- Might miss edge cases you forgot
- You never saw it catch the bug

Test-first forces you to see the test fail, proving it actually tests something.

**"I already manually tested all the edge cases"**

Manual testing is ad-hoc. You think you tested everything but:
- No record of what you tested
- Can't re-run when code changes
- Easy to forget cases under pressure
- "It worked when I tried it" â‰  comprehensive

Automated tests are systematic. They run the same way every time.

**"Deleting X hours of work is wasteful"**

Sunk cost fallacy. The time is already gone. Your choice now:
- Delete and rewrite with TDD (X more hours, high confidence)
- Keep it and add tests after (30 min, low confidence, likely bugs)

The "waste" is keeping code you can't trust. Working code without real tests is technical debt.

**"TDD is dogmatic, being pragmatic means adapting"**

TDD IS pragmatic:
- Finds bugs before commit (faster than debugging after)
- Prevents regressions (tests catch breaks immediately)
- Documents behavior (tests show how to use code)
- Enables refactoring (change freely, tests catch breaks)

"Pragmatic" shortcuts = debugging in production = slower.

**"Tests after achieve the same goals - it's spirit not ritual"**

No. Tests-after answer "What does this do?" Tests-first answer "What should this do?"

Tests-after are biased by your implementation. You test what you built, not what's required. You verify remembered edge cases, not discovered ones.

Tests-first force edge case discovery before implementing. Tests-after verify you remembered everything (you didn't).

30 minutes of tests after â‰  TDD. You get coverage, lose proof tests work.

## Common Rationalizations

| Excuse | Reality |
|--------|---------|
| "Too simple to test" | Simple code breaks. Test takes 30 seconds. |
| "I'll test after" | Tests passing immediately prove nothing. |
| "Tests after achieve same goals" | Tests-after = "what does this do?" Tests-first = "what should this do?" |
| "Already manually tested" | Ad-hoc â‰  systematic. No record, can't re-run. |
| "Deleting X hours is wasteful" | Sunk cost fallacy. Keeping unverified code is technical debt. |
| "Keep as reference, write tests first" | You'll adapt it. That's testing after. Delete means delete. |
| "Need to explore first" | Fine. Throw away exploration, start with TDD. |
| "Test hard = design unclear" | Listen to test. Hard to test = hard to use. |
| "TDD will slow me down" | TDD faster than debugging. Pragmatic = test-first. |
| "Manual test faster" | Manual doesn't prove edge cases. You'll re-test every change. |
| "Existing code has no tests" | You're improving it. Add tests for existing code. |

## Red Flags - STOP and Start Over

- Code before test
- Test after implementation
- Test passes immediately
- Can't explain why test failed
- Tests added "later"
- Rationalizing "just this once"
- "I already manually tested it"
- "Tests after achieve the same purpose"
- "It's about spirit not ritual"
- "Keep as reference" or "adapt existing code"
- "Already spent X hours, deleting is wasteful"
- "TDD is dogmatic, I'm being pragmatic"
- "This is different because..."

**All of these mean: Delete code. Start over with TDD.**

## Example: Bug Fix

**Bug:** Empty email accepted

**RED**
```typescript
test('rejects empty email', async () => {
  const result = await submitForm({ email: '' });
  expect(result.error).toBe('Email required');
});
```

**Verify RED**
```bash
$ npm test
FAIL: expected 'Email required', got undefined
```

**GREEN**
```typescript
function submitForm(data: FormData) {
  if (!data.email?.trim()) {
    return { error: 'Email required' };
  }
  // ...
}
```

**Verify GREEN**
```bash
$ npm test
PASS
```

**REFACTOR**
Extract validation for multiple fields if needed.

## Verification Checklist

Before marking work complete:

- [ ] Every new function/method has a test
- [ ] Watched each test fail before implementing
- [ ] Each test failed for expected reason (feature missing, not typo)
- [ ] Wrote minimal code to pass each test
- [ ] All tests pass
- [ ] Output pristine (no errors, warnings)
- [ ] Tests use real code (mocks only if unavoidable)
- [ ] Edge cases and errors covered

Can't check all boxes? You skipped TDD. Start over.

## When Stuck

| Problem | Solution |
|---------|----------|
| Don't know how to test | Write wished-for API. Write assertion first. Ask your human partner. |
| Test too complicated | Design too complicated. Simplify interface. |
| Must mock everything | Code too coupled. Use dependency injection. |
| Test setup huge | Extract helpers. Still complex? Simplify design. |

## Debugging Integration

Bug found? Write failing test reproducing it. Follow TDD cycle. Test proves fix and prevents regression.

Never fix bugs without a test.

## Testing Anti-Patterns

When adding mocks or test utilities, read @testing-anti-patterns.md to avoid common pitfalls:
- Testing mock behavior instead of real behavior
- Adding test-only methods to production classes
- Mocking without understanding dependencies

## Final Rule

```
Production code â†’ test exists and failed first
Otherwise â†’ not TDD
```

No exceptions without your human partner's permission.

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Run the artifact tests and archive a timing-free LF transcript."""

from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import re
import subprocess
import sys
from pathlib import Path


MODULES = ("artifacts.test_verify_pisot_pumping",)


def main() -> int:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    parser.add_argument(
        "--output",
        type=Path,
        default=Path(__file__).with_name("unittest_output.txt"),
    )
    args = parser.parse_args()
    root = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]
    display_command = "python -m unittest -v " + " ".join(MODULES)
    command = [sys.executable, "-m", "unittest", "-v", *MODULES]
    completed = subprocess.run(
        command,
        cwd=root,
        capture_output=True,
        text=True,
        check=False,
    )
    transcript = completed.stdout + completed.stderr
    print(transcript, end="")
    stable_transcript = re.sub(
        r"^(Ran \d+ tests?) in [0-9.]+s$",
        r"\1",
        transcript.replace("\r\n", "\n"),
        flags=re.MULTILINE,
    )
    archive = (
        f"Python version: {sys.version.split()[0]}\n"
        f"command: {display_command}\n"
        "random seed: none (deterministic)\n\n"
        + stable_transcript
    )
    args.output.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    args.output.write_text(archive, encoding="utf-8", newline="\n")
    return completed.returncode


if __name__ == "__main__":
    raise SystemExit(main())

   1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
   2: """Finite checks for recurrent-numeration pumping and local obstructions.
   3: 
   4: This is not a proof of language immunity.  It checks the exact matrix identity
   5: used in the proof on explicit recurrences, synchronized multi-block return
   6: periods, a local prime-layer isolation certificate, prescribed-depth quotient
   7: congruences, and a finite induced divisibility tree.  It also searches for the

  56:     recurrence: tuple[int, ...]  # U_(n+d) = sum recurrence[j] U_(n+j)

  61:     def dimension(self) -> int:
  62:         return len(self.recurrence)

  65:     def trailing_coefficient(self) -> int:
  66:         return self.recurrence[0]

  86: class PumpWitness:
